#118. Brexit work travel rules need urgent improvement, say UK business leaders

CBI says restrictions on cross-Channel work trips are hindering investment and trade between UK and Europe

Business chiefs have called on the UK government and the European Commission to urgently ease Brexit restrictions on business travel for cross-Channel work trips.

A group representing industry bosses said it wants commitments from Andy Burnham and European political leaders that the restrictions will be one of the priorities in 2027 for furthering ties between the two sides, after a summit in Brussels this week.

The Confederation of British Industry (CBI), which represents some of Britain’s largest companies, welcomed the prime minister’s recent calls for a reset with Europe, designed to reverse some of the damage to Britain’s economy caused by Brexit.

But it said Brexit restrictions, which include making employees obtain work permits for travel, are deterring cross-border investors and hampering trade at the very time both sides are trying to build resilience against China.

Sean McGuire, the CBI’s director of Europe and international, said discussions should have been a priority in the talks this week, after a UK-EU summit in 2025 held by Keir Starmer also referenced improving business mobility.

“Very little has been achieved. In fact, practically nothing has been achieved. The emphasis was put on youth mobility,” said McGuire.

EU diplomats have said in the past that the UK pushed hard for improving business mobility but was met with resistance from the bloc, where member states see British services – a big export particularly in the financial sector – as a threat.

McGuire said that energy, defence and car industry bosses say they are being hampered by the inability to travel freely in Europe to service or run multinational operations.

He said the issue should be of interest to all sides as they build on multiple existing laws to protect European supply chains.

“Now that we’ve got youth mobility potentially in the Friday deal, we should move seriously to address the kind of business mobility, which would apply to both the goods sector and the services sector,” he said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

British citizens travelling to the EU post-Brexit are restricted to 90 days at a time out of a maximum of 180 days a year, limiting companies’ ability to promote employees and travel.

“If you are a CEO of a multinational you can very quickly clock up 90 days,” McGuire said.

Separately, work permits have to be obtained for each country that a worker travels to. That can affect the servicing of equipment, particularly in engineering, where products that cost millions such as lifts, escalators and factory machines come with service and maintenance contracts.

“There is a big difference between irregular migration, which needs to be tackled, and business mobility, which is bringing investment into the UK and vice versa,” he said.

While an agreement is close on youth mobility, Burnham faces the same roadblock when it comes to opportunities for under-30s, who will be offered a three-year visa scheme to work, travel or study.

“It is a trick because the UK is negotiating with Brussels but work permits are a national competency,” said one EU diplomat.

But McGuire said there is a quick fix for business mobility, involving changes to the annex of the EU-UK trade and cooperation agreement, which allows the two sides to expand visa-free business travel across more industries without reworking the main treaty.

The CBI said it is also “disappointed” that no progress has been made on mutual recognition of qualifications allowing British or EU professionals to practise in each other’s territories as they did before Brexit.

Sources said there was a view in Brussels that mutual recognition of qualifications was more beneficial to the UK than to the EU, causing energy around the talks to drain away.

#117. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#115. Has the 'Parthenon puzzle' been solved? The long quest to reassemble lost eastern pediment

The depiction of the birth of the goddess Athena above the Parthenon's entrance is considered a masterpiece of ancient art. Now, a German research team has pieced together all the surviving fragments.

The 2,500-year-old Parthenon is ancient Greece's most important temple and is regarded as one of the greatest masterpieces of the High Classical period of Greek art.

Dedicated to Athena, the city's patron goddess, it towers over Athens from its position on the Acropolis — a powerful symbol of the city's importance.

The Parthenon had a lasting influence on Western ideas of humanity and art. The sculptures adorning its pediments, created under the direction of the legendary sculptor Phidias, are considered unparalleled examples of proportion and grace. They have influenced artists from ancient Rome through Neoclassicism to the modern era.

The east pediment above the temple entrance depicted the birth of the goddess Athena from the head of her father Zeus, surrounded by the Olympian gods. A total of 39 figures adorned the roughly 29-meter-long work of art. At its center stood a colossal Zeus figure, measuring 3.8 meters in height.

For centuries, scholars have puzzled over exactly what the arrangement looked like, as the pediment was destroyed during the Christianization of Greece.

After decades of painstaking detective work and with the help of modern technology, archaeologist Vinzenz Brinkmann and his team have managed to piece together all the surviving fragments of the Parthenon's east pediment.

Brinkmann heads the Antiquities and Asia department at Frankfurt's Liebieghaus sculpture collection. "We're very, very confident that we've solved the mystery," he told DW.

The results are now on display in an exhibition at the Liebieghaus titled "Athena, the Stage is Yours: The Parthenon Puzzle."

Only around 20% of the original marble sculpture's volume is known to have survived. The fragments are scattered across various European museums, with most located in Athens. The British Museum also holds numerous pieces.

Brinkmann and his wife, Ulrike Koch-Brinkmann, have spent more than 40 years working on this ancient puzzle, with so many of its pieces missing.

And they were far from the first to attempt it: "Already in the 19th century, many scholars studied the subject and developed well-founded theories based on their deep knowledge of ancient sources," Brinkmann explains.

In 1910, a study documenting the many fragments held by the British Museum was published. The research team was able to build on that earlier work, but progress remained slow for years.

Then, in 2014, a visit to the Hermitage Museum in St. Petersburg provided Brinkmann with a crucial clue. There, he encountered a Roman sarcophagus featuring a relief of Athena's birth. "That's when it suddenly clicked: They're all there!"

Nine such sarcophagi are known to exist, almost all of them in Rome. Each depicts a different section of the Parthenon's east pediment.

Brinkmann emphasizes that other scholars had already noticed this connection in the 19th century. "But we really seized on the idea and never let go."

The researchers also benefited from 21st-century technology: 3D scanners and printers. Brinkmann and his team visited numerous destinations to examine around 60 original fragments at different locations — traveling light. An iPhone was all they needed to scan the pieces.

Digitally fitting the fragments together led to another important discovery. "We know these figures we're putting together!" Brinkmann recalls thinking at the time.

The Romans had repeatedly made individual copies of all the sculptures from the pediment. "These copies matched the fragments perfectly. Suddenly, we found ourselves surrounded by old acquaintances we had never suspected had come from the pediment." This finally enabled the researchers to reconstruct all the figures and their original arrangement.

Using a 3D printer, the team created a 4-meter-long model at a scale of 1:7.7. For the exhibition, they also produced life-size 3D prints of the most important fragments and mounted them on panels depicting the complete figures at their original size. An animation brings the reconstructed composition to life, digitally assembling the fragments to reveal how the east pediment once looked.

Alongside works from the Liebieghaus collection, the exhibition features loans from other institutions, including the Vatican Museums and the Louvre. Objects from the Acropolis Museum in Athens had originally been expected as well. But Greece withdrew its participation after learning that the British Museum was also planning to lend fragments to the Frankfurt exhibition. The Liebieghaus ultimately decided to forgo the British-owned fragments, too.

There is a reason for this controversy. Britain holds a substantial portion of the Parthenon frieze as well as fragments from both pediments.

Greek authorities have been campaigning for their return for decades.

In Britain, the 56 sections of the frieze are known as the "Elgin Marbles," named after Lord Elgin, the British ambassador to the Ottoman Empire in Constantinople.

In the early 19th century, Elgin had sculptures removed from the exterior of the Parthenon on the Acropolis in Athens. They were taken to Britain with the consent of the Ottoman Empire, which ruled Greece at the time.

Britain maintains that the sculptures were legally acquired.

For years, the dispute remained deadlocked. Since the Labour Party came to power in Britain, there has been some movement toward resolving it. But apparently not enough to persuade Greece to lend artworks to an exhibition in which the British Museum might also participate.

The exhibition "Athena, the Stage is Yours. The Parthenon Puzzle" runs through July 25, 2027, at the Liebieghaus in Frankfurt.

This article was originally written in German.

#114. The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

Brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei

In the space of just five years, Anthropic has gone from unknown start-up to tech giant, worth more than a trillion dollars.

The company behind the AI platform Claude was formed by seven people who left rival OpenAI in 2021 and, in a matter of hours, will become multi-billionaires - on paper at least.

Anthropic is floating the business on the US stock exchange and will sell its shares at between $70 - $78 each.

If they go for the highest price, the founders' shareholdings will be worth at least $18.5bn each.

So, who are these people, all in their thirties and forties, who are about to become incredibly wealthy?

In 2020, Dario Amodei was working for OpenAI, the firm that brought generative artificial intelligence to a mainstream audience, when it launched Chat-GPT.

He decided he wanted to do things his own way, and left, taking his sister Daniela, and five colleagues with him.

Amodei grew up in San Francisco, impressing school friends not just with his academic prowess but also his speed on the running track, and his preoccupation with philosophical and technological questions including the potential of machines to gain consciousness and human longevity.

Initially he studied physics at Princeton and Stanford. But following his father's death from hepatitis C, he switched to biology and biomedical sciences.

Amodei did stints at the Chinese tech giant Baidu and Google before joining OpenAI.

But he began to worry that some people within the company were putting profit before safety, prompting the decision to jump ship.

Now 43, he is Anthropic's chief executive, and the person who, in his sister's words, makes the company "ambitious".

Dario and Daniela Amodei, were close growing up and later shared a flat together in San Francisco with other friends. She followed him into OpenAI and joined him when he left to found Anthropic.

She and her brother have always genuinely liked each other, she says, but the only thing she ever did first, was when he followed her in becoming vegetarian.

Amodei studied English Literature at the University of California Santa Cruz, going on to work for Democrat representative Matt Cartwright.

She has also worked at financial services platform Stripe, where she was head of risk operations.

The 39-year-old is president of Anthropic, manages the executive team, and leads the firm's research, engineering, product, governance and commercial work.

Tom Brown, now 39, followed the Amodei siblings when they left to found their new venture.

He led GPT-3 engineering at OpenAI, and prior to that was at Google on its DeepMind project.

Brown established his entrepreneurial credentials co-founding an app called "Grouper" that helps set up drinks between groups of friends.

He has supported US President Donald Trump's stance on datacentres, arguing that they are "an enormous source of prosperity".

Now as chief compute officer at Anthropic he is responsible for how the company secures and uses its computing resources.

Jack Clark, is another arts graduate among the original start-up's founding members and the only one who is not from North America.

Originally from Brighton in the UK, he studied English Literature and Creative Writing at the University of East Anglia and became a technology journalist, writing for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

He was caught up in the excitement of artificial intelligence and joined OpenAI, where he worked initially in communications, later becoming its policy director.

Now, 37, Clark is head of public benefit at Anthropic. He also leads the Anthropic Institute, an AI think tank.

Jared Kaplan, 42, is chief science officer at Anthropic, and heads its technical research. He is also an associate professor at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore.

He holds a PhD in physics from Harvard University.

His work on scaling laws - which describe mathematical patterns that show that bigger AI models trained on more data tend to be more consistent - has helped provide AI firms with a framework for predicting AI behaviour, according to Anthropic.

Chris Olah is described as "interpretability research lead" primarily focused on how AI models work with each other.

But according to the New York Times, Olah has spent much of the last year convening meetings with faith leaders from across the religious spectrum.

Described by the newspaper as "boyish" but with "the zeal of an evangelist" the 34-year-old is reportedly exploring the relationship between morality and AI.

He previously led interoperability research at OpenAI, and prior to that was a research scientist at Google Brain.

The Canadian left Toronto University early, but later secured a Thiel Fellowship of $100,000, which is a scholarship to help gifted people move into research without a degree.

Sam McCandlish, 36, takes less of a public-facing role than the other founders, but like them will own around 1.6% of the company after its shares go on sale. As a result he too will see his stake crystallise into a hugely valuable asset.

McCandlish is Anthropic's chief architect, and helps shape and implement the company's policy designed to mitigate the risks of AI. Prior to this he worked on safety at OpenAI.

He has a physics PhD from Stanford, and maths and physics degree from Brandeis University outside Boston.

#112. David Lammy urges Andy Burnham to fight next election on rejoining EU

Former foreign secretary’s intervention comes as prime minister prepares for major UK-EU summit in Brussels

Andy Burnham should fight the next election on a promise to rejoin the EU, the former business secretary Peter Kyle has said, with voters given a referendum on the outcome of any deal.

The Labour MP, a key member of Keir Starmer’s cabinet, said the “only way through” the challenges of the domestic political debate was for the government to offer voters the choice to accept or reject any agreement.

His intervention comes as the prime minister prepares for a major UK-EU summit in Brussels on Friday, at which he hopes to secure agreements on food and farming and emissions trading.

Burnham has said there was “no rush” to decide on the UK’s longer-term relationship with the EU, as he came under pressure from his own party to press the brakes on the pace of the promised pursuit of closer ties.

Despite his suggestions at Labour conference, he is now not expected to set out the full options for a closer EU relationship until his 10-year plan in December – although he has denied he is “stepping back” from commitments.

Kyle is the latest senior Labour figure to publicly wade into the Brexit debate after the former foreign secretary David Lammy said the party should go into the next election clear that its intention was to rejoin.

Rachel Reeves, the former chancellor, has also argued Britain’s future should be back in the bloc, arguing that leaving had made the UK “poorer, weaker and less secure”.

Kyle, who was the author of a key amendment on a second vote during the original parliamentary battles over Brexit, said a “halfway option” such as joining the customs union or single market was a “non-starter”.

Writing on his Substack blog, he said: “The only way through I see is for Labour to have in its next manifesto a commitment to begin negotiations for rejoining the EU, alongside a promise that any final decision to rejoin would be subject to a referendum on the actual terms.

“A campaign based on a complex halfway option, weighted with sovereignty implications, would be the worst of all worlds. Politically, it would be a non-starter. It would be seen as a stepping stone to full membership which, for most supporters, it would be. So let’s get quickly to the real choice we face: in or out.”

Kyle said his Labour manifesto proposal would, in contrast, give voters two distinct propositions, the status quo or the known terms of rejoining, “with an electorate informed and able to scrutinise both”.

He criticised the new pro-Brexit campaign group Stay Out, which was set up by architects of the Vote Leave campaign and has claimed rejoining the EU would cost Britain £750m a week. Kyle accused it of “making random claims” about the cost of rejoining that were “as good as fictitious”.

The former minister urged his fellow pro-Europeans not to suggest that every problem the UK faced could be solved by rejoining, saying there was “an awful lot of fixing, building and potential” for the UK economy that wasn’t based on it.

“We must act with urgency on these [issues] and not be seduced into believing an easy route to growth can be found in the single market that avoids tough decisions now,” Kyle said.

Lammy, who served as deputy prime minister under Starmer, said Burnham’s Liverpool conference speech “blew the door wide open” on the issue.

“Burnham’s Labour should go into the next election clear that its intention is to rejoin the EU,” Lammy wrote in the Times. “Forming the next government would give it the mandate to begin formal negotiations.

“Once a deal is agreed, this should be put to the people. Unlike the last referendum, the public should have the opportunity to decide between two clearly defined choices.”

On Tuesday, Burnham denied the debate on rejoining had become “toxic”, reiterating that he would stick to Labour’s red lines before the general election, but the public would be “in charge” of what came next.

#111. Pakistan's solar revolution is so successful the national grid is lagging behind

Faizal Shahzad wants to buy several more solar panels to make his home in rural Punjab fully self-sufficient

Jewellery, glimmering silverware, sofas and an air-conditioning unit. Three months after his wedding, Faisal Shahzad's home in rural Punjab is bursting with gifts from his bride's family, as part of the dowry.

But the most prized possession is tucked under the bed: a 340W solar panel.

"In the summer we ran it from 08:00 in the morning until the evening," he says, adding they didn't need to use grid power at all during the day. "We ran our air cooler on it all day."

Pakistan has seen a boom in imported solar panels, which glisten from the rooftops of homes and hospitals; from farmers' fields to industrial complexes.

From 2018 to 2025, the share of electricity generated from solar energy in the country rose from 1% to 28%, according to Pakistan-based think tank Renewables First, with some seven million households now owning panels.

People used to gift motorbikes or other household items at weddings, Shahzad says. "Now they give a solar panel and an air cooler. It has almost become a must."

Alongside the solar panel he still uses the national grid to meet his energy needs, particularly as he lives in a house with his family and brothers' families, who often use multiple appliances simultaneously. But in a country where the average wage is just under 40,000 Pakistani rupees (Â£110; $145) per month, bills are becoming unaffordable, rising to 15,000-18,000 rupees.

This year, COP31 is pushing the world to move away from fossil-powered vehicles, boilers and other greenhouse gas-emitting equipment. And while Pakistan has become a poster child for solarisation, the national grid system is experiencing knock-on eï¬ects.

The big pivot to solar wasn't driven by government policy but by individual Pakistani households. Most people we spoke to said they installed panels to escape soaring electricity prices from an often unreliable national grid.

Electricity tariffs have more than doubled in a few years, says Muhammad Hassan Dajana, industry liaison at Renewables First, while the cost of Chinese solar panels has "decreased by 93%".

"People have just found another way to be able to survive," says Energy Minister Awais Ahmad Leghari. "It's a very healthy thing. We have adapted to the situation. The people have reacted and responded in a very economically viable and efficient manner."

While the solar boom brings clear benefits, particularly for a country that imports huge amounts of fuel, there are worries too.

Pakistan needs to avoid the so-called utility death spiral.

When people buy less energy from the national grid, fewer customers are left to cover the fixed costs of maintaining it. That can force prices up - which in turn encourages even more people to switch to solar.

Over time, this can lead to a two-tier system in which those who can't afford to buy panels face ever-rising bills, while the national grid becomes an increasing burden on the state.

"You'll have a white elephant in the form of a state-owned enterprise that requires a lot of public funding to just stay afloat," says Dajana.

More and more households are installing solar panels, as seen here in Karachi, but Pakistanis still need energy from the national grid

Pakistan's government says the costs are already being felt.

"In just the past two years, the exodus from the grid has cost the rest of the consumers more than $500 million [Â£378m] a year," says Leghari. "That's a very big burden."

The government doesn't want to pull people away from solar, he explains, but wants the grid to be more competitive to be able to offer better energy prices.

Private power producers, which feed into the grid, have been heavily criticised inside Pakistan for locking the country into costly, inflexible contracts. Producers themselves point to broader structural problems like poor grid maintenance as contributing to higher bills. Leghari says the government has renegotiated most of the contracts and is now focused on making the grid more efficient with better storage, less waste and more innovation.

Leghari wants the country to run on "up to 90% clean power" by the end of 2036 - including renewables and nuclear - with dependence on imported energy reducing from 26% to 4%.

The solar power boom in Pakistan and the Philippines

Cheap solar panels, mainly imported from China, are transforming lives and slashing electricity bills across Asia.

But as the government tries to improve the grid, solar is still expanding with widespread adoption in industry.

Kohinoor Textiles Mills in Islamabad, which is powered by a sea of huge blue solar panels, is one example. Utilities manager Sabawoon Khan describes them as his "baby", pointing at construction work levelling the ground for more panels.

When the sun shines, the mills' hundreds of machines are powered entirely by solar energy, Khan says. The heavy-furnace-oil engines they once relied on sit mothballed in a warehouse.

But there are limits. When it's too cloudy or polluted, and during the night, the company takes more energy from the national grid. In the coming weeks, like other firms, it will start using industrial batteries which can store excess solar energy.

"We needed to reduce our energy costs and this was one way to do it," says Taufique Sayeed Saigol, the mills' CEO, who adds the plan is not to disconnect from the grid altogether.

"The state unfortunately doesn't have the resources so we can't really say that we blame everything on the state. We do some of the things ourselves."

Rows of large solar panels power this textiles factory in Islamabad for large parts of the day

Critics accuse the government of moving the goalposts on solar power. Previously, households could feed excess electricity into the grid and earn credits, which were then used to offset the cost of grid power when the sun wasn't shining. That policy changed earlier this year.

"Initially their policy was very helpful," says Arif Ehsan Malik, president of Faisalabad's chamber of commerce. "But then the policy changed. Now they have started purchasing at a lower rate and selling back at a higher rate."

Malik says that makes a big difference for small and medium businesses. Leghari counters that the costs of the initial model didn't add up; the electricity the solar producers put into the system was much cheaper than the electricity they ended up taking out of it.

"Those profits were coming at the cost of the rest of the consumers of the grid. Had we not changed those regulations, the rest of the consumers would have been burdened by at least 150 to 200 billion rupees a year, say, two years from now."

Muhammad Hassan Dajana from Renewables First believes that with the right policy, Pakistan could become a global example of a successful energy transition

Despite admitting that the first wave of solar adoption was a "surprise" to the government, Leghari says he has no fear that solar could outrun their plans, saying it has been incorporated into their 10-year forecasts and the numbers leaving the grid have slowed.

Pakistan has big ambitions for solar; there are plans to open solar battery factories around the country and encourage more electric vehicles.

"There are two paths ahead," Dajana says. One is a future where solar and the grid work in parallel, demonstrating how a gradual energy transition can succeed. The other is where Pakistan becomes a cautionary tale, burdened with a costly, declining grid.

Shahzad's solar ambitions for his home in rural Punjab certainly have not slowed.

"We plan to get another 10-15 panels and install them on the roof, then connect a full system with batteries and back up.

"The plan is to be fully self-sufficient."

#110. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#107. 'Coalition of the Willing' meets in Berlin: In an era of waning support, Europe remains committed to Ukraine

The "Coalition of the Willing" supporting Ukraine is gathering in Berlin. One of the key issues under discussion is whether Europe will be able to play a role in future negotiations over the country's future.

Once again, Berlin is rolling out the red carpet, and once again the focus is on Ukraine: around 20 heads of state and government, primarily from across Europe, are set to gather in the German capital on Thursday (November 19). They will discuss the future of the Eastern European country that has been under attack from Russia since February 2022 and is now facing yet another harsh winter.

Extensive security measures have already been announced for Berlin's government district, pointing to the presence of high-ranking participants. It remains unclear whether Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy will be among them. What does appear certain, however, is the attendance of chief US negotiators Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, according to several media outlets.

The "Coalition of the Willing" is a group of 36 countries determined not to be deterred from supporting the nation under attack. Unlike other international forums, the group is focused not only on how to support Ukraine militarily and financially in the here and now.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Led by Germany, France, and the United Kingdom, the coalition is also prepared, in principle, to help monitor a possible ceasefire within Ukraine itself and to contribute to the complex security guarantees the country would then require, should such a scenario come to pass. The effort dates back to March 2025, when the countries involved first met in London. Now they are gathering in Berlin.

But what can the coalition realistically achieve? Stefan Meister of the German Council on Foreign Relations (DGAP) is not overly optimistic. In an interview with DW, he said: "The fundamental problem is that Europe has yet to put forward a proposal. There is no plan outlining what security guarantees Europe would offer, what troops it would be prepared to deploy, what measures it could provide to safeguard a ceasefire, or where it would be willing to make compromises."

Nevertheless, the meeting comes at a moment when some observers believe there may finally be movement toward ending the war. During talks in Abu Dhabi in the United Arab Emirates a few days ago, Russia, Ukraine, and the US managed to agree only on a prisoner exchange. It was a modest step forward.

Further talks are planned, and it has been a long time since the prospects appeared as favorable as they do now for Europe to secure a role in future decisions on war and peace in Ukraine.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

It has repeatedly been argued, both by US officials and by those in power in Russia, that Europe does not speak with a single voice.

Unsurprisingly, this has sparked a debate among EU heads of state and government over who, exactly, could represent Europe in such talks. Could it be German Chancellor Friedrich Merz of the center-right Christian Democratic Union (CDU)? After the EU summit in Dublin, Ireland, on Friday, he said: "For the first time, concrete peace talks are expected to take place in the coming weeks with Europe represented. Germany has a seat at the table, that is absolutely clear."

But what exactly did that mean? That Germany alone would speak for all of Europe? Hardly. As was also made clear in Berlin on Monday, Europe would be represented by the E3 countries, namely Germany, France, and the non-EU member United Kingdom, joined by Poland and Italy. Official EU representatives, including European Council President António Costa, would also take part.

Stefan Meister of the DGAP believes the debate says a great deal about the pressures Europe itself is under: "Europe's positioning has more to do with domestic political considerations than with developments in the negotiations. Everyone needs to show that they are participating in the talks in some way. Elections will be held next year in France and Poland, and it is still unclear whether French support for Ukraine will continue in its current form after that."

German Deputy Government Spokesperson Sebastian Hille, meanwhile, sought to downplay the debate on Monday over a possible role for Friedrich Merz as chief negotiator. At the federal government's regular press briefing, he said: "This is not about discussing individuals. What matters is that Putin finally understands that he must come to the negotiating table so that we have a chance of achieving peace in the first place."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

US negotiator Jared Kushner, the son-in-law of President Donald Trump, struck a rather different tone last week. Speaking in Abu Dhabi, he said: "If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiations."

Government spokesperson Hille responded on Monday: "Europe's strength lies precisely in the unity and cohesion of many countries. We should not allow ourselves to be drawn into debates that others want to frame around this question." Hille emphasized that the situation remained unchanged: peace in Ukraine could only be achieved step by step. Europe, too, would need stamina, perseverance, and a willingness to undertake the painstaking work required to bring it about.

Declining support for Ukraine in German polls

European supporters of Ukraine are also under pressure as public opinion on the issue is shifting, including in Germany. While a broad majority favored helping defend the country in the immediate aftermath of Russia's invasion in the spring of 2022, support has since fallen markedly.

According to the latest ARD DeutschlandTrend survey on the issue, conducted in September 2026, 52% of eligible voters still support weapons deliveries to Ukraine, while 42% believe such support has gone too far. That is the highest level of opposition recorded since the start of the war.

A further study conducted for public broadcaster WDR in November 2026 found that support for Ukraine has become a more polarizing issue in Germany than immigration. According to the survey, 54% of respondents favor continued support for Ukraine, while just under 29% would prefer a rapprochement with Russia, despite its war of aggression against Ukraine.

This article was originally written in German.

#106. Can a US-backed mineral highway bring peace and prosperity to DR Congo?

The Lobito Corridor links DR Congo's mineral-rich south to Angola's Atlantic coast through the Benguela Railway and the Port of Lobito

As trains carrying thousands of tonnes of copper from the Democratic Republic of Congo roar through her community, 20-year-old Manuela Julia is not thinking about critical minerals or billion-dollar investments.

She is thinking about getting around more easily.

The railway runs through Catumbela, near the Angolan city of Lobito, on the Atlantic coast.

For Julia, it has made travelling cheaper and more convenient.

"Before, I had to use a taxi, which is much more expensive. Now it is cheaper and easier to travel," she says.

But the railway has ambitions far bigger than improving local transport.

It is part of the Lobito Corridor, a US-backed effort to secure access to Africa's critical minerals and promote regional economic cooperation, which Washington hopes will help bring peace to conflict-torn eastern DR Congo.

Under agreements brokered by US President Donald Trump, DR Congo and neighbouring Rwanda have committed to greater economic cooperation, including more transparent mineral supply chains and regional infrastructure development.

The wider plan envisages linking these initiatives to the railway, which transports minerals from Central Africa to Western markets via Angola, rather than east to China as most do currently.

The hope is that formalising the mineral trade and encouraging investment could help curb illicit markets that have helped finance armed groups and fuel instability.

Manuela Julia says the railway has made travelling around easier

But can a railway built to move minerals to global markets also deliver peace and prosperity?

The Lobito Corridor links DR Congo's mineral-rich south to Angola's Atlantic coast through the Benguela Railway and the Port of Lobito.

Its importance has grown as demand for copper and cobalt rises, driven partly by the global transition to cleaner energy, as both are critical ingredients for all kinds of electrical components.

"The timing matters," says Johannesburg-based political risk analyst Wellington Muzengeza

"Electrification and the energy transition have increased the strategic importance of copper, cobalt and reliable mineral supply chains."

DR Congo is the world's largest producer of cobalt and one of its biggest copper producers, along with neighbouring Zambia. The US sees the corridor as a means to diversify mineral supplies and reduce reliance on China, which dominates much of the world's processing capacity.

A US-DR Congo strategic partnership identifies the route from the Congolese town of Sakania to Lobito as a priority for mineral exports, while the economic framework agreed by DR Congo and Rwanda envisages connecting regional infrastructure and trade initiatives to the corridor.

The project has attracted over $750m (Â£570m), including funding backed by the US International Development Finance Corporation and the Development Bank of Southern Africa to upgrade railway tracks, signalling systems and trains.

The railway, built in 1902 under Portuguese colonial rule, was originally designed to transport goods and minerals from Central Africa to the Atlantic coast. After years of disruption during Angola's civil war, it was rebuilt and fully reopened in 2015.

The US first got involved in the project under former President Joe Biden but backing has continued under Trump.

At Lobito's mineral terminal, the renewed ambitions are already visible.

When the BBC visited, a train carrying about 12,000 tonnes of copper cathodes from DR Congo had arrived. Workers moved the refined copper sheets into a warehouse before they were loaded on to ships bound for international markets, mostly the US.

The cargo is handled by Lobito Atlantic Railway (LAR), which has a 30-year concession to operate freight services along the corridor and manage the mineral terminal.

The Lobito Atlantic Railway has a 30-year concession to operate freight services along the corridor

Chief operating officer Nicholas Gregoir says freight volumes have more than doubled since the company took over in January 2024.

LAR expects to transport about 200,000 tonnes of copper through the corridor in 2026.

For mining companies, the railway offers a shorter route to international markets.

"The Lobito Corridor is the shortest route for both Zambia and the DRC," to the Atlantic, says KÃ¡tila Vanessa Monteiro da Silva Neto, executive director of the Lobito Port Authority.

Zambia, Africa's second-largest copper producer, also plans to connect its Copperbelt to Lobito through a proposed 830km (515 mile)-railway.

But a more efficient route to market does not automatically translate into economic transformation.

"Joining a corridor is not the same as industrialising," Muzengeza says. "The test is not simply whether Zambia gains another route to the sea. It is whether that route helps build a stronger economy."

Some Angolans believe the railway's greatest value could lie in what it offers businesses and communities beyond mining.

"I call it a corridor of development," says businessman AdÃ©rito Areias.

The railway passes through some of Angola's most productive agricultural regions.

Better transport links could help farmers reach larger markets and reduce losses.

"If there isn't an artery carrying products from the interior to where there is demand, those products simply rot where they are produced," Areias says.

But the challenge is turning that ambition into reality.

The Port of Lobito handled about 1.8 million tonnes of cargo in 2025, of which around 158,000 tonnes was minerals. Its mineral terminal can handle up to 3.6 million tonnes annually.

"The port already has the capacity," says Neto. "What we need now is sufficient railway capacity and rolling stock."

For residents along the route, the benefits have been immediate.

State-owned railway operator Caminho de Ferro de Benguela (CFB), which runs passenger services, carried a record 1.6 million passengers in 2025.

LAR says it directly employs nearly 1,000 people, 98% of them Angolan.

"This job allows me to support my family," says Domingos Canhama, a father of four and a forklift operator at CFB.

"I can put food on the table, maintain a home and help support my father," he says.

Muzengeza argues that the bigger opportunity is developing local businesses in engineering, maintenance, warehousing and agricultural processing.

"Businesses in Angola need access to the railway, not just proximity to it. That means feeder roads, electricity, storage and predictable services," he says.

But can minerals and their movement along the railways line help deliver peace to eastern DR Congo, where the battle for control of mineral resources has fuelled decades of conflict?

Domingos Canhama, a forklift operator at the Lobido railway company says the job has helped him support his family

Most recently, this has seen the M23 rebel group seize control of many mineral-rich areas.

The Congolese government says neighbouring Rwanda is backing the M23 in order to gain access to DR Congo's mineral wealth. Despite overwhelming evidence, Rwanda denies backing the M23.

The Trump-backed agreements between DR Congo and Rwanda seek to promote transparent mineral supply chains, cross-border investment and regional infrastructure, with the aim of ensuring mineral wealth does not finance armed groups.

"The US wants reliable access to minerals essential to defence, advanced technology and energy systems, while reducing dependence on supply chains in which China has substantial influence," says Muzengeza, the political risk analyst

"Lobito is therefore more than a railway but an infrastructure that can reshape trade routes and commercial power."

At a meeting in Paris in November, DR Congo urged the US to turn its strategic partnership into tangible investments, arguing that success should be measured by completed projects rather than signed agreements.

"We want the partnership to support â local mineral processing, electricity access for households and businesses and peace in the east," said DR Congo's Economy Minister Daniel Mukoko Samba.

Even if more minerals move through formal channels, better infrastructure alone would not resolve the political and security tensions driving the conflict.

There is also the question of who captures the wealth. Much of the copper and cobalt transported from DR Congo through Lobito is destined for processing and manufacturing outside Africa.

While Angola can earn transit fees and create logistics jobs, much of the higher-value activity takes place elsewhere.

"When you export raw materials, you are also exporting jobs and knowledge," Angola's Foreign Affairs Minister TÃ©te AntÃ³nio tells the BBC.

"We should not export lithium but export batteries," he says, arguing that the corridor should help develop local processing and manufacturing rather than simply accelerate raw material exports.

Back in Catumbela, such debates can feel distant from everyday life.

For Julia, the railway already means cheaper journeys and easier movement.

But its ultimate success will depend on whether it can deliver more than faster mineral exports.

Whether it can also help secure lasting peace in eastern DR Congo, where the minerals are mined, remains the bigger test.

#105. Labour faces double blow over UK fossil fuel projects

Big North Sea operator EnQuest says it is quitting oil basin, while SSE threatens to close Yorkshire gas storage site

The energy secretary has been dealt a double blow after two UK companies signalled plans to pull their investment from a series of major fossil fuel projects over government policies.

One of the North Sea’s biggest independent companies, EnQuest, said on Wednesday it was quitting the oil basin to focus on making better returns in Asia, blaming the “uncertain investment environment within the UK”.

Separately, the energy company SSE told investors that next summer it would close one of the country’s largest gas storage sites, which is on the North Sea coast, unless the government intervened to offer support amid weak market conditions.

The tacit criticism is expected to pile pressure on Miatta Fahnbulleh, who became the energy secretary in July, to clarify the government’s plan to secure the UK’s gas supplies as households face a rise in energy bills this winter because of soaring gas costs.

EnQuest set out plans to sell its entire portfolio of UK oilfields to the biggest North Sea producer, Neo Next+, for up to $960m (£730m), weeks after the company urged the chancellor, John Healey, to use his budget in November to reform the industry’s tax regime.

The UK oil and gas industry warned after the budget that the Treasury’s decision to leave its planned North Sea tax changes until the end of the decade was “continuing to drive energy investment abroad”.

The British oil company BP is also in the process of exiting the North Sea after announcing in the summer that it would end six decades of production from the basin by selling its entire business there.

The EnQuest deal marks “another significant company exiting the UK continental shelf”, according to James Hosie, an equity analyst at Shore Capital. He said the company had opted to leave after 16 years as a leading independent operator in the basin “rather than hold out for an improvement in the UK regulatory and fiscal regime for upstream oil and gas”.

SSE told investors that while its Atwick gas storage site in the north of England had a “strategic importance” to the energy system as the North Sea’s domestic production declined, it was “increasingly clear that existing market arrangements do not support their continued operation”.

Closing the Atwick facility, near Hornsea on the East Yorkshire coast, would remove about 309m cubic metres of gas, or about 1.5 days’ worth of Great Britain’s gas usage, from the UK’s storage capacity.

The site makes up a fifth of the country’s onshore gas storage capacity, or 7% when including the storage tanks at the UK’s liquified natural gas terminals and the Rough site, also at risk of closure unless its owner, Centrica, the parent company of British Gas, can agree a long-term financial support deal with the government.

The renewed concerns over supplies emerged on the same day that the National Energy System Operator (Neso) warned in a second consecutive annual report that the UK was at risk of a gas supply crisis as the North Sea’s production winds down.

The report echoed concerns that while the UK has enough access to global gas markets to cover demand when weather is normal, if even a single piece of Britain’s ageing gas infrastructure suffered an “unlikely” outage during a very cold spell, then supplies could fall short of demand. This would lead to emergency measures such as shutting off gas supplies to factories, power plants and – in extreme scenarios – homes.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Energy companies typically rely on stockpiling gas in their storage facilities during the summer, when prices are usually lower because of weaker demand, before selling the gas at a profit during winter when demand for energy usually lifts market prices.

However, the gas price increases triggered by the Iran conflict this year and by Russia’s war on Ukraine in 2022 has upended normal market patterns, leading to historic market price highs during the summer that have made it uneconomical to stockpile gas ahead of winter.

As a result, countries across Europe have entered the cooler months of the year with gas stores at their lowest level in more than a decade.

Britain already stores less gas than some of its European neighbours and is instead more reliant on liquefied natural gas imports. The closure of a storage facility such as Atwick could leave the UK even more exposed to price shocks on global markets.

Last month, Fahnbulleh gave the final approval for the Jackdaw gasfield, which began producing gas for the first time earlier this week. She is expected to make a decision on whether to consent to oil production from the Rosebank field before the end of the year.

The government was approached for comment.

#102. Steve Rosenberg: Historic talks with Xi and Trump are a coup for Putin

Nearly five years after the full-scale invasion of Ukraine turned him into a pariah, Putin is back on the global stage

But Thursday's mini summit of Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping already looks like a win for the Kremlin.

Think back to 2022. After Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine President Putin was an international pariah. Western leaders were determined to isolate him and his country over the war in Ukraine.

Fast forward to 2026: Vladimir Putin has secured an historic meeting in Shenzhen. For the first time in history, the leaders of China, Russia and the United States will sit down â just the three of them â for talks.

That puts Putin exactly where he wants to be: at the top table of global politics, rubbing shoulders with the leaders of the two great powers.

It allows him to boast that Western pressure on Russia has failed.

"We see such examples in the failed attempts to isolate our country, Russia, which has faced unprecedented sanctions pressure," Putin told a business forum of the Asia-Pacific Economic Co-operation organisation.

That was followed by a meeting with Xi Jinping.

But the Kremlin says the "showstopper" event in Shenzhen will be Putin's trilateral with Xi and Trump.

It allows him to play global statesman, even as the Russian military continues to pound Ukrainian towns and cities, and the Kremlin continues to reject the idea of an unconditional, comprehensive ceasefire.

One short meeting is unlikely to reshape the world. But it's the optics that are key. The picture. The photo-op. Three leaders, three big powers, discussing big issues.

In a way, Vladimir Putin will use this meeting to send a holiday postcard to the rest of the world. Instead of "Wish you were here", he'll sign it: "Look: I'm here."

But what of those who are not at the table?

Among European leaders, and especially in Ukraine, there is concern that the three-way meeting might produce more than just a pretty picture, though not in a good way for Kyiv.

Donald Trump has already said that in Shenzhen he wants to discuss his "favourite subject" - the war in Ukraine. The Kremlin would likely welcome an opportunity to argue its case to Trump and Xi without Ukrainian officials or European leaders in the room.

Last month Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky expressed concern that the Putin-Trump-Xi meeting might resemble the Yalta Conference of 1945. That is when the leaders of the Soviet Union, Britain and the United States â Joseph Stalin, Winston Churchill and Franklin D Roosevelt - met to re-organise post-World War Two Europe.

"We really don't want this to turn into 'Yalta 2, 3, 4' with a redistribution of influence," said Zelensky.

It is unclear whether China would want the Ukraine war on the agenda in Shenzhen. In its official statements on the upcoming trilateral meeting, Beijing has talked more generally about the need to discuss "safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability".

In Shenzhen, Russia will make the most of the optics. But a diplomatic victory is not the same as a military one.

Nearly five years after President Putin launched his so-called "special military operation", Russia is still struggling to win its war on Ukraine.

Over this time, it has suffered huge losses on the battlefield.

And the Ukrainian strike on the road bridge linking Russia to the Crimean Peninsula â carried out on the eve of the summit - was a blow to Moscow's prestige. The attack severely damaged part of the multi-billion-dollar bridge built by Russia after it annexed Crimea from Ukraine.

The damage is not only a logistical headache for the Russian authorities.

It is a reminder that the Kremlin's "special operation" has not gone at all to plan.

The Shenzhen summit may give Vladimir Putin a big stage, and a bright spotlight.

It does not necessarily bring him closer to winning the war.
