#101. Fried chicken and a chat with an old friend: How Marcos catered to Trump

China’s leader, Xi Jinping, earlier hugged President Trump on the tarmac as he arrived for a regional summit in Shenzhen. They are expected to hold a trilateral meeting on Thursday with President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

Peter BakerLily KuoPaul SonneKeith Bradsher and Leo Sands

Peter Baker, Paul Sonne and Keith Bradsher reported from Shenzhen, China. Lily Kuo reported from Taipei, Taiwan, and Leo Sands from London.

President Trump, Xi Jinping of China and Vladimir V. Putin of Russia greeted each other with smiles and handshakes on Wednesday in Shenzhen, China, hours after Mr. Trump received a personal airport welcome from Mr. Xi that included a hug.

The shows of friendliness by the three world leaders, on the eve of a trilateral summit, contrasted sharply with their divergent stances on some of the world’s most pressing issues, including nuclear arms control, China’s access to advanced A.I. chips and the future of the war in Ukraine.

On Thursday, the three are expected to participate in the first-ever gathering of the leaders of the United States, China and Russia, when at least some of those issues could be on the agenda. The summit will cap Mr. Trump’s whirlwind trip to Asia, which has been shadowed by the Nvidia scandal, trade conflicts and critical infrastructure breakdowns at home.

The atmosphere at the wider summit with other leaders on Wednesday was sometimes tense. Mr. Xi barely offered a smile to Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi of Japan, with whom he is in a bitter, yearlong diplomatic spat. Mr. Trump and Prime Minister Mark Carney of Canada, who have an uneasy relationship as they tussle over trade, had their first in-person meeting of the year. Days earlier, Mr. Carney angered the Trump administration by repeating that he would look elsewhere, including to China, if Mr. Trump did not lift tariffs on Canada’s auto exports.

Earlier in the day, Mr. Xi met Mr. Trump at the airport in Shenzhen, which he did not do when the president visited Beijing in May. But Mr. Trump made a point of going to Joint Base Andrews outside Washington to greet Mr. Xi in September, an uncommon move for an American president and the first time Mr. Trump had done so for a foreign visitor. In effect, Mr. Xi returned the gesture.

The red-carpet greeting on the tarmac — with a crowd of people in colorful costumes waving flags and flowers and chanting “warm welcome” in Mandarin — came shortly after Mr. Xi met with Mr. Putin, who had arrived earlier. The Chinese leader had sent a lower-ranking official to greet Mr. Putin but used their subsequent meeting to hail the Chinese-Russian relationship as “steadfast as a rock through wind and rain.”

The two encounters underscored Mr. Xi’s efforts to balance relations with Russia and the United States. While Russia has become a close partner in recent years and Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin have developed a bond, China has a far more extensive economic relationship with the United States.

Mr. Trump will also meet separately with Mr. Xi on Thursday, his third gathering this year with the Chinese leader after his visit to Beijing and the lavish state visit at the White House. Although Mr. Trump once regularly lashed out at China over its trade practices, he has become more intent on forging friendly ties between the world’s two largest economies.

The visit, however, may have a see-no-evil, hear-no-evil quality because Mr. Trump has said he does not intend to raise issues of Chinese interference in American affairs. The chief executive of Nvidia, Jensen Huang, was forced to resign last week after revelations of an extramarital affair with a businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence determined was spying for China. But Mr. Trump has said he will not discuss the matter with Mr. Xi.

Manila stop: Mr. Trump’s Asia tour began in Manila, where he joined a meeting of Southeast Asian nations. He also met with an old friend, Imelda Marcos, the former first lady of the Philippines and the mother of the country’s current president. Read more ›

Russian show of force: Russian strategic nuclear bombers landed in Vietnam around when Mr. Putin landed in China, an apparent signal before the trilateral talks with Mr. Trump that Moscow remained the world’s largest nuclear power. Read more ›

Fast-food diplomacy: President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines said he had fried chicken from Jollibee, a chain that is considered a national treasure, delivered to Mr. Trump during his overnight stay in Manila. Read more ›

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia held talks for more than two hours with China’s leader, Xi Jinping, in Shenzhen on Wednesday, a day before the two leaders were scheduled to meet with President Trump.

As Mr. Putin landed in China, Russia engaged in a purposeful show of force by flying strategic nuclear bombers to Vietnam for the first time since the Soviet era. Mr. Putin spent much of the day demonstrating that despite his isolation from many Western nations over their opposition to the war in Ukraine, he remains on good terms with much of the rest of the world.

Mr. Xi greeted Mr. Trump at the airport when the American president arrived in Shenzhen, the first time he has greeted a foreign dignitary on the tarmac in more than a decade as China’s leader. In contrast, Mr. Xi sent a longtime deputy, Cai Qi, to welcome Mr. Putin, who arrived in the early hours of the morning.

By arriving before Mr. Trump, Mr. Putin got extra face time with Asian leaders ahead of the scheduled trilateral meeting on Thursday between the United States, China and Russia. That meeting will be the first involving the leaders of those three countries.

No relationship has proved more critical for Mr. Putin than his bond with Mr. Xi. Mr. Putin met with the Chinese leader for at least the fourth time this year on Wednesday, holding large group talks with the Russian and Chinese delegations in Shenzhen before moving on to a smaller “working breakfast” with top aides.

As a major purchaser of Russian energy and supplier of electronics and consumer goods, China has served as a lifeline for wartime Russia. Western companies pulled out of the Russian market after the invasion, and Moscow scrambled to adapt in the face of heavy sanctions.

At the outset of the talks with Mr. Xi, Mr. Putin underscored the rapidly increasing cooperation between Russia and China, noting that tourist flows had increased 43 percent since the countries introduced visa-free travel late last year.

Mr. Putin said both Russia and China were taking “constructive approaches to shaping a more equitable, multipolar world order.” That is the Russian leader’s favored oblique terminology for a world no longer dominated by the United States.

Mr. Putin also met with Vietnam’s leaders before joining a dinner at the summit, where he was welcomed by Mr. Xi and shook Mr. Trump’s hand.

The scheduled trilateral meeting comes as Moscow continues to play hardball with the Trump administration over talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine.

Mr. Trump’s envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff last week sought Mr. Putin’s approval to add European officials to talks involving the United States, Russia and Ukraine, but the Kremlin rebuffed the request in public comments after the meeting.

Outside the trilateral meeting, it was not immediately clear how extensive talks between Mr. Trump and Mr. Putin would be. The Kremlin spokesman, Dmitri S. Peskov, told state news media that there most likely would not be time for full-fledged talks between Mr. Trump and Mr. Putin but said they would find time to discuss Ukraine and possibly Iran.

At the banquet, President Trump was seated with his back to reporters at the center of the table and Xi Jinping on his left. When reporters were ushered out, the two leaders were conversing through interpreters.

About an hour later, the president departed and returned to his hotel for the night.

President Trump, President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia and Xi Jinping, the leader of China, shook hands before the start of a banquet that includes the national leaders of the APEC summit. Trump, Putin and Xi plan to hold a trilateral summit on Thursday.

Xi Jinping, standing at a podium adorned by flowers, lifted a glass of red wine and made a toast at the gathering of leaders. With so many visitors, roads are closed in many places across central Shenzhen.

Amid a bitter trade war between their countries, President Trump and Prime Minister Mark Carney met in-person on the sidelines of the APEC summit for the first time since last year. In a statement, Mr. Carney’s office said that the two leaders “discussed the work underway to reach a mutually beneficial trade agreement” but offered no further details.

Unlike Trump, Putin was not greeted at the airport by Xi when he arrived in the middle of the night. Cai Qi, Xi’s longtime deputy, met the Russian leader.

The atmosphere was tense. The handshake was quick.

President Xi Jinping of China and Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi of Japan met briefly on Wednesday at the opening of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum in Shenzhen, China, their first encounter since the start of a bitter diplomatic feud a year ago.

Mr. Xi offered Ms. Takaichi a slight smile as she made her way to a banquet with other world leaders. He did not appear to respond when she said a few words to him, before they posed for a photo with Mr. Xi’s wife, Peng Liyuan.

Experts said the interaction was unlikely to signify any serious thaw in relations, which have been at a low point since China reacted furiously last year to a comment Ms. Takaichi made about Taiwan.

Tetsuo Kotani, a professor at Meikai University in Chiba Prefecture in Japan, said that Ms. Takaichi seemed “eager to make the most of this opportunity” in Shenzhen. Mr. Xi, he said, “maintained a businesslike demeanor.”

“I don’t think there’s any hope this time around for improved relations,” Mr. Kotani said.

As the summit got underway, there were reminders of the frosty state of relations. While other world leaders received warm welcomes at the Shenzhen airport — a crowd of dancing young people with flowers greeted the South Korean leader, for example — there were no such gestures for Ms. Takaichi, according to Japanese news reports.

The dispute began last year, when Ms. Takaichi, a longtime critic of China, told Japan’s Parliament that any attempt by Beijing to blockade or seize Taiwan could constitute a “survival-threatening situation” for Japan, implying a potential military response. Beijing considers Taiwan, a self-governed democracy, part of its territory.

China unleashed a wave of reprisals against Japan, urging tourists to avoid the country, limiting the export of critical minerals and restricting Japanese seafood imports. At the same time, Chinese officials and commentators have hardened the language they use to describe Japan, with some seeking to belittle Ms. Takaichi by omitting her title in official settings.

China’s punishments have started to squeeze Japan. The decline in tourism has hurt sales at some duty-free stores, hotels and restaurants. Japan could soon run low on supplies of some rare earths — minerals used to make a range of essential technologies, from M.R.I. machines to drones — according to analysts.

Chinese officials and scholars have suggested that anything short of Ms. Takaichi retracting her comment on Taiwan would not satisfy Mr. Xi.

Shao Yuqun, director of the Institute for Taiwan, Hong Kong and Macau Studies at the Shanghai Institutes for International Studies, said that Mr. Xi was responding in part to intense anger among the Chinese public over Ms. Takaichi’s remark.

Ms. Shao added that finding a way to improve relations with Japan was “certainly not the priority” in Shenzhen.

Ms. Takaichi has been working to accelerate Japan’s defense buildup to counter a volatile security environment, including threats from China, North Korea and Russia.

There is little appetite among the Japanese public to curry favor with Beijing and Ms. Takaichi is unlikely to give in to Chinese demands to retract her remark on Taiwan. As she enters her second year in office, she enjoys relatively robust public support, and won a historic supermajority in the lower house of Japan’s bicameral Parliament in February.

The meeting had echoes of an encounter in 2014, when Mr. Xi offered an icy handshake to Shinzo Abe — then Japan’s prime minister and a mentor to Ms. Takaichi — at the Great Hall of the People in Beijing during another moment of tension between the two countries. Mr. Abe was eventually able to get more time with Mr. Xi, and relations between the two countries improved somewhat.

After their encounter on Wednesday, Mr. Xi and Ms. Takaichi are unlikely to have an extended meeting in Shenzhen, officials from both countries said.

Ms. Takaichi is expected to face another challenge on Thursday: a potential sit-down on the sidelines of the summit with Russia’s president, Vladimir V. Putin. The meeting would take place as Mr. Putin is escalating pressure on Japan. He recently seized Russian assets of Japan Tobacco, detained a Japanese journalist and visited islands claimed by both Moscow and Tokyo.

Japan will also be watching the outcome of a first-ever trilateral gathering of the leaders of the United States, China and Russia.

Tobias Harris, the founder of the advisory firm Japan Foresight, said Tokyo hopes the meeting is “light on substance.”

“You can’t rule out the eventual possibility of some attempts at a big grand settlement that is prejudicial to the global order that Japan prefers,” he said.

Verena HölzlAie Balagtas See and Jason Gutierrez

What President Trump appeared to lack in enthusiasm, his hosts in the Philippines made up for in hospitality by catering to his preferences during a brief state visit.

The president stopped in Manila on his way to a historic meeting with the autocratic leaders of Russia and China in Shenzhen, just as the summit of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, or ASEAN, was wrapping up.

He did not even go to the summit venue; Southeast Asian leaders met him at the hotel where he was staying.

When President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines rushed to leave a news conference after wrapping up the ASEAN summit, he told reporters: “I cannot keep the president of the United States waiting. Sorry.”

For Mr. Marcos and much of the region, flattering Mr. Trump is part of a strategy of balancing the United States and China.

Although Mr. Marcos has called for a reset of relations with China, tensions remain high with Beijing over territorial claims in the South China Sea.

“Whether they like it or not, the Philippines is a traditional U.S. ally,” said Georgi Engelbrecht, a Philippines analyst at the International Crisis Group, a think tank. “China will never trust it strategically.”

The Philippines and the United States are bound by a 75-year-old mutual defense treaty.

In remarks before their bilateral meeting on Wednesday, Mr. Marcos seemed to make an effort to use language that would please his guest, referring to A.I. as “super intelligence,” a term Mr. Trump has made mandatory for his government.

Tapping into Mr. Trump’s predilection for fast food, Mr. Marcos said he had sent Mr. Trump fried chicken from the Philippine chain Jollibee. “It’s providing extra energy for all the work that is in front of you,” Mr. Marcos said to the president at their meeting the next day.

During his roughly three-minute speech, Mr. Marcos brought up Mr. Trump’s “brief stop” in Manila at least six times.

For his part, Mr. Trump was also complimentary, telling Mr. Marcos that he was well acquainted with his parents, the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos and his wife, Imelda Marcos, who came to be known for her shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

“You have very good genetics, I can tell you that,” Mr. Trump told Mr. Marcos at a dinner with Southeast Asian leaders on Tuesday.

“Personal chemistry and rapport between leaders is important,” said Lucio Pitlo III, the president of ThinkSea Consulting, a Philippine think tank. “But leveraging them to tangibly advance national interests is the more crucial metric.”

He said Mr. Marcos’s efforts to have good relations with Mr. Trump had paid off in the past, for example, in a proposed U.S. tariff rate that was half as much as in other parts of the region.

Filipinos have traditionally viewed the United States, its former colonial ruler, more favorably than they view China. But punishing tariffs and higher fuel prices as a result of the war in Iran have been difficult for the country to absorb.

After his meeting with Mr. Marcos, Mr. Trump announced that the two sides had signed “a very big ⁠trade deal.” Mr. Marcos’s office described it a an “understanding on key areas of economic cooperation.”

Personal diplomacy was on display again later in the day. Mr. Trump briefly spoke with Imelda Marcos, 97, who is now rarely seen in public. As he left, she blew him a kiss from her wheelchair.

Xi Jinping told President Trump that he took the unusual move for a Chinese leader of greeting him at the airport because the American president had done the same for him when he visited the United States in September. “China is a nation of etiquette with a tradition of reciprocity,” Xi said, according to Chinese state media.

President Trump is arriving at the summit as his envoys, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, face headwinds in trying to negotiate a peace deal with Russia to end the war in Ukraine. Kushner and Witkoff went to Moscow last week to seek Putin’s approval to add the Europeans to talks, but Kremlin officials publicly rebuffed the request.

Putin’s arrival was accompanied by the same orchestra playing the trepak, or Russian dance melody, from Pyotr Tchaikovsky’s “The Nutcracker.”

Xi Jinping, the Chinese leader, welcomed guests in Shenzhen, where President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia arrived and shook hands with President Trump.

President Trump walked into the event on a red carpet as a Chinese orchestra played a rendition of “America the Beautiful.”

Prime Minister Mark Carney of Canada has advanced his goal of reducing Canada’s economic reliance on the United States during a weeklong swing through Asia, emerging with trade deals with China and the Philippines.

But he was also presented with an unexpected opportunity to improve relations with Washington. On Wednesday, Mr. Carney announced that he would meet with President Trump at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, leaving open the prospect of Canada starting to mend the rift with its neighbor.

“We have long said, and believed and still believe, that there is a mutually beneficial agreement between Canada and the United States in the context of the new U.S. trade policy,” Mr. Carney told reporters. “We’ll have discussions on that. And, we will do what’s necessary in order to achieve that deal, but only if that deal that is in the interest of Canadians.”

Hours later, Mr. Carney’s office issued a brief statement confirming that the two leaders met and “discussed the work underway to reach a mutually beneficial trade agreement.” It offered no details.

Before Wednesday, Mr. Carney’s aides said there was no expectation of a meeting with Mr. Trump in China. At the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in South Korea last year, the two men only spoke informally during a dinner.

How much progress, if any, will result from the meeting is unclear. Before the encounter, Mr. Carney described dealing with the Trump administration on trade as a “two steps forward, one step back type thing.”

Since his return to office, Mr. Trump has imposed a variety of tariffs on a wide range of Canadian products. While those tariffs have been effectively suspended for many goods that qualify as being made in North America, the suspension has not applied to the key auto, steel and aluminum industries. Their products face tariffs of up to 50 percent.

Negotiations between the two countries collapsed in August with each side pointing fingers at the other for the failure.

At the beginning of this month, Mr. Carney said that talks would restart after Mr. Trump said the United States would back off on some secondary issues that were irritants for Canada, including requirements relating to the promotion of Canadian and French language content on digital streaming services.

There has been little apparent activity since then but Mr. Carney said on Wednesday that “we have been making progress.”

Much more obvious has been the progress Mr. Carney has made with China. Before he took office, diplomatic incidents — including what was widely seen in Canada as the hostage-taking of two Canadians by China in 2018 — and Canada’s decision to follow the United States with 100 percent tariffs on Chinese-made electric vehicles had brought relations between the two countries to a near standstill.

But this week, at his second formal meeting with President Xi Jinping of China this year, Mr. Carney walked away with a second reduction in Chinese tariffs aimed at a variety of farm and fish products. The deal effectively marked the end of the trade war sparked by the auto tariff, Mr. Carney said, opening the door to an expanded trade relationship between the countries.

Mr. Carney also repeated this week that if Canada were unable to persuade Mr. Trump to end tariffs on its auto exports, it would look to China, Japan and Europe to keep Canada’s auto industry afloat.

That suggestion drew criticism from Peter Navarro, a senior adviser to Mr. Trump.

“Are u really threatening to play China off the U.S.?” Mr. Navarro said in a social media post. “This is offensive in the extreme. You should be far more worried China is going to eat Canada alive — as it is already doing.” Pete Hoekstra, Washington’s ambassador to Canada who has frequently offended Canadians, shared the post.

On Wednesday, however, Mr. Carney dismissed suggestions that Canada’s renewed relationship with China was poisoning its economically vital relationship with the United States.

“In my conversations with the president, he’s tried to understand where we are with China, where we’re going with China,” Mr. Carney said. “I shouldn’t speak for him, but in his hierarchy of issues, it’s not the top issue at all.”

But selling the benefits of China back home may not be so easy. A recent poll found that only 14 percent of Canadians want closer trade ties with China. By contrast, 58 percent back Mr. Carney’s push for a closer relationship with the European Union.

After noting that Canadian fishermen and farmers strongly favor more trade with China, Mr. Carney downplayed the extent of the trade relationship.

“Relative to many other countries, our trade with China is quite modest and the breadth of it is quite modest.”

Yuri Ushakov, a top aide to President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, told Russian state news media that Putin may speak with President Trump at a dinner for leaders in Shenzhen this evening before their formal meeting on Thursday.

When President Trump arrived in Shenzhen on Wednesday, Xi Jinping was waiting for him at the bottom of the steps descending from Air Force One.

It was the first time that Mr. Xi had personally received a foreign dignitary at the airport in more than a decade as China’s leader. Visiting leaders are usually welcomed by senior Chinese officials and greeted by Mr. Xi at the Great Hall of the People in central Beijing.

Mr. Xi’s appearance, while unusual, was likely about reciprocating Mr. Trump’s hospitality. When Mr. Xi traveled to Washington for a state visit in September, Mr. Trump greeted the Chinese leader at Joint Base Andrews. That was the first time the U.S. president had personally greeted a foreign visitor upon their arrival.

After Air Force One landed in Shenzhen for the last stop on his whirlwind trip to Asia, Mr. Xi rolled up to the tarmac in a black luxury sedan made by the Chinese carmaker Hongqi, or Red Flag. He walked toward the president’s plane along a red carpet flanked by young men and women cheering and waving American and Chinese flags. After Mr. Trump walked down the stairs from his plane toward Mr. Xi, the two shook hands. Mr. Trump patted Mr. Xi on the back before the two embraced.

Wu Xinbo, the dean of the Institute of International Studies at Fudan University in Shanghai, said the leaders’ personal receptions reflected a shift in the U.S.-China relationship to focus on “respect, fairness, and reciprocity.”

Mr. Wu said that Mr. Xi’s presence was also likely a message of support for the American president, to show that China will “still treat him seriously and would like to continue to work with him,” after the Republican Party suffered a bruising defeat in the recent midterm elections.

The high-level reception accorded to Mr. Trump was a step up from the welcome given to President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, who arrived earlier on Wednesday. Mr. Putin was greeted by Cai Qi, a longtime deputy of Mr. Xi and one of the most influential officials in the Chinese leader’s inner circle. Mr. Cai is a member of the powerful Politburo Standing Committee, the Chinese Communist Party’s top echelon.

When Mr. Trump visited China in May, he was greeted by China’s vice president, Han Zheng, a ceremonial figurehead who often attends formal diplomatic events. When he visited in 2017, the president was greeted by Yang Jiechi, China’s then-top diplomat and a member of the Politburo, the party’s second-most powerful body.

In 2009, when Mr. Xi was vice president and already seen as the likely successor to China’s leader at the time, Hu Jintao, Mr. Xi waited at the airport to greet President Barack Obama on his arrival in Beijing.

David Pierson contributed reporting from Hong Kong.

Paul Sonne reported from Shenzhen, China, and Damien Cave reported from Ho Chi Minh City.

Source: Satellite image by Planet Labs, captured on Tuesday.

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia touched down in Asia in recent hours. So did two of his nuclear-capable bombers.

Mr. Putin arrived early Wednesday in Shenzhen, China, for a regional economic meeting and a trilateral summit with President Trump and President Xi Jinping of China. Satellite images showed that two Russian Tu-95MS strategic nuclear bombers had landed on Tuesday at Da Nang Airport in nearby Vietnam alongside two long-range transport aircraft.

It was the first time that Russian strategic nuclear bombers had deployed to Vietnam since the Soviet era, a pointed demonstration of Russia’s nuclear might and global reach ahead of the high-level talks.

The surprise arrival of the Russian aircraft in the region caused a scramble among American allies in Asia.

Japan dispatched jets after Russian planes were detected in Japanese airspace near Okinoshima, an island in the south of Japan, and Tokyo registered its discontent with the Russian authorities. South Korea also deployed fighter jets; the authorities there said the Russian planes entered South Korea’s Air Defense Identification Zone, where Seoul closely monitors foreign aircraft, but not its sovereign airspace.

Russia lags both China and the United States significantly in economic output, technological innovation, population size and trade flows, making Mr. Putin’s participation in the trilateral summit scheduled for Thursday to some degree conspicuous.

But the one critical area where Russia has retained its lead is in nuclear weapons. Russia boasts the world’s biggest nuclear arsenal, which Mr. Putin has brandished as a reason the world must continue to accord Moscow a certain level of deference and respect. Russia has also developed a range of new nuclear weapons systems in recent years.

The Tu-95MS is a nuclear-capable bomber with intercontinental range. Russia has also deployed the bombers in its war against Ukraine to launch conventional missiles.

Taiwan on Tuesday said it detected two sorties of Russian military aircraft flying in airspace east of the island. Gen. Huang Chih-wei, the Vice Chief of the Taiwanese military’s General Staff, said that “the actions of the Russian aircraft undermine regional peace and stability and are detrimental to maintaining the status quo of peace and stability across the Taiwan Strait.”

The Russian and Vietnamese authorities did not make any public statements about the arrival of the bombers. Mr. Putin met with Vietnam’s leader, To Lam, in Shenzhen on Wednesday on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, which Mr. Trump and Mr. Xi are also attending.

Russia and Vietnam have a longstanding relationship that dates back to the Cold War, and Russia still supplies the vast majority of Vietnam’s military equipment.

For Vietnam, the decision to welcome the Russian nuclear bombers shows China that Vietnamese leaders have a range of possible military partners to push back against any aggressive moves by Beijing. Vietnam has also deepened its ties with the United States in recent years.

But it is a careful balancing act for the Vietnamese.

Vietnam allowed Russia to refuel its bombers from Cam Ranh Bay in 2014 as they patrolled the area around Guam, but the refueling was done midair and the bombers did not land on Vietnamese soil. At the time, the United States protested the use of Cam Ranh Bay, a former American and then Soviet base, for the refueling.

Reporting was contributed by Javier C. Hernández in Tokyo, Chris Buckley in Taipei, Taiwan, Choe Sang-Hun in Seoul and Tung Ngo in Hanoi, Vietnam.

When Imelda Marcos and Donald J. Trump partied in New York in the 1990s, she was the exiled former first lady of the Philippines, the wife of an ousted dictator, and he was a real estate mogul facing multiple bankruptcies.

They met again in Manila on Wednesday in vastly different roles. Mr. Trump, 80, is, of course, the president of the United States. And Ms. Marcos, 97, is the mother of the president of the Philippines, her family ensconced again in Malacañang Palace.

They had a brief conversation, according to a video posted on social media by a White House aide. It showed President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines walking Mr. Trump to where Ms. Marcos was waiting in a wheelchair.

“Mom,” Mr. Marcos, 69, said as he ushered Mr. Trump toward her. He has said that Mr. Trump knows his mother “very well.”

Ms. Marcos has rarely been seen in public after she was hospitalized with pneumonia in 2024.

“Welcome to the Philippines,” she told Mr. Trump. She handed him a gift and blew him a kiss as he walked away with her son.

“She’s always the most beautiful,” Mr. Trump said.

Ms. Marcos wielded immense power when her husband was president and came to be known for her flamboyant lifestyle, shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

Mr. Trump, who was in the Philippines for a brief state visit, has made clear that he admires the Marcos family, part of his well-known affinity for autocratic leaders. On Tuesday night, he said to Mr. Marcos: “I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great.” He went on, “I believe in genetics. They did a good job. You have very good genetics, I can tell you that.”

Earlier on Wednesday, Secretary of State Marco Rubio told reporters in Manila that Trump was willing to meet with Kim Jong Un, the leader of North Korea, to resolve issues. However, Rubio did not say whether a meeting between Trump and Kim was likely to happen.

“The president has never viewed meeting and talking to someone as a concession,” Rubio said. “How are you going to solve problems with people you have disagreements with if you don’t speak to them?”

Xi’s decision to personally greet Trump at the airport is unusual. He did not come himself when Trump visited Beijing in May. But Trump made a point of going to Joint Base Andrews outside Washington to greet Xi in September, a rare move for any American president and the first time Trump had done so for a foreign visitor.

Trump was greeted upon his arrival in Shenzhen by Xi, in an unusually high-level welcome ceremony. Foreign leaders are usually received by senior Chinese officials, not Xi himself.

President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines said on Wednesday that he had fried chicken delivered to President Trump during his overnight stay in Manila.

It wasn’t just any fried chicken. It came from Jollibee, a chain that is considered a national treasure in the Philippines.

“Since you’re strapped for time, I thought a taste of Manila might be good. So last night, I asked my staff to have some Jollibee delivered as a late night snack for you and your delegation,” Mr. Marcos said in his remarks before a meeting with Mr. Trump.

It was not clear if Mr. Trump actually ate any.

Mr. Marcos’s gesture was not only a matter of national pride, but also appeared to be a nod to Mr. Trump’s well-documented fondness for fast food. Mr. Trump, a self-described germaphobe, has said that he favors fast food from chains like McDonald’s because of their speed, consistency and hygiene.

In April, he received two McDonald’s bags delivered by DoorDash to the Oval Office, in an event the White House had staged to promote a Trump administration initiative that removed taxes on overtime and tips.

“I like cleanliness, and I think you’re better off going there than maybe someplace that you have no idea where the food’s coming from. It’s a certain standard,” Mr. Trump said in a 2016 interview with CNN. Later that year, he posted a photo of himself on social media enjoying a bucket of Kentucky Fried Chicken on his private plane.

Mr. Marcos told Mr. Trump on Wednesday that Jollibee had surpassed Kentucky Fried Chicken in popularity in the United States. The claim was dubious but Mr. Trump replied: “Oh, wow, fantastic.”

While KFC still has many more stores than Jollibee in the United States, the Filipino chain has promoted its fried chicken as America’s best since at least 2022, citing food critics’ rankings and three consecutive wins in a USA Today readers’ poll.

On its home turf, Jollibee far outpaces American fast-food chains in store count and revenue. To compete with Jollibee in the Philippines, McDonald’s has altered its local menu to offer a sweeter hamburger sauce, fried chicken and rice, and Filipino-style sweet spaghetti.

Jollibee has expanded beyond the Philippines by acquiring food brands in countries such as the United States, China and South Korea, including the Colorado-based burger chain Smashburger and the Hong Kong dim sum chain Tim Ho Wan.

Mr. Marcos’s shout-out came as Jollibee is exploring a Hong Kong Stock Exchange listing for its international business, after initially saying it would list in the United States. The company’s founder and chairman, Tony Tan Caktiong, said in September that the change reflected Asian investors’ familiarity with the brand.

Jollibee has not opened any stores in Washington, though there are several in New York, Mr. Trump’s home state, and Florida, where he spends a lot of time.

Xi said in a meeting with Putin that relations between China and Russia have remained as “steadfast as a rock through wind and rain,” according to Chinese state media. Xi called for the two sides to advance “world multipolarity” and the “democratization of international relations,” terms Xi uses to describe a world order in which the United States is less dominant.

At a lunch with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines on Wednesday, Trump said that the two countries had unexpectedly signed a new trade deal. Marcos’s office later said that the deal would cover energy and food security, without providing further details.

“We didn’t know that was going to take place, but it took place very quickly and it’s a magnificent deal for both,” Trump said during the lunch. He later said on social media that the deal would benefit American farmers.

Prime Minister Mark Carney of Canada told reporters that he would meet with Trump on Wednesday in Shenzhen to discuss the trade dispute between the two countries.

China warned on Tuesday that it could hit back after the Trump administration this week proposed a rule that would restrict China’s access to advanced chips through data centers outside its borders.

China’s ministry of commerce said the rule went against the terms that China and the United States had agreed to in a fragile truce that expires in two months. “If the US persists, China will take countermeasures,” a ministry spokesman said.

China experts warned that the rule, the latest in a series of escalatory moves from both sides, would make it harder for Xi and Trump to come to any meaningful agreement to deescalate conflict between the world’s biggest economies.

In a sign of Putin’s importance to Xi, the Russian leader was greeted at the airport in Shenzhen on Wednesday by Cai Qi, one of the seven members of the elite Politburo Standing Committee. That appears to make Cai the highest-ranking official to greet a foreign leader at APEC so far ahead of Trump’s arrival, according to a review of Chinese state media.

Cai is considered one of the most influential figures in Xi’s inner circle, having served as a deputy to the Chinese leader when he was a provincial official. He accompanied Xi to Washington in September.

This is not the first time Cai has welcomed a foreign leader on the tarmac. Last year, he and Wang Yi, China’s top diplomat, greeted North Korea’s Kim Jong-un when he landed in Beijing.

Putin and Xi are expected to tour the campus that Moscow State University, Russia’s top university, set up in Shenzhen with the Beijing Institute of Technology. The leaders agreed to establish it a decade ago. The Shenzhen outpost looks like a mini version of the Moscow university, complete with a replica of its Stalin-era tower.

Putin and his team held talks with Xi followed by a smaller working breakfast. They have met dozens of times; this is at least their fourth in-person meeting this year. The talks lasted more than two hours, according to the Russian state news agency Tass.

World leaders coming to Shenzhen this week are arriving in an almost spotless, ultramodern metropolis, its streets bedecked with pastel banners but mostly emptied of the crowds typical of many Chinese cities.

Shenzhen lies slightly closer to the Equator than Havana, providing a balmy venue for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation gathering. Under sunny skies with mild temperatures, a festive mood prevails in the city, which bears a strong resemblance to Miami — only much larger and with taller, newer high-rises.

Schools in Shenzhen have closed this week, many residents have left town and local authorities have instructed companies to allow those employees who are still here to work from home. The result has been a holiday atmosphere of sorts in a city with a tech sector known for its punishing “996” work schedules: 9 a.m. to 9 p.m., six days a week.

Youthful volunteers — mainly students at local universities — stand on street corners wearing bright red vests, having been offered academic credit in exchange for being trained as guides. Thousands of gawkers have been promenading past the large conference center that will host heads of state from across the Asia Pacific region, taking photos of themselves in front of the flower gardens inside the fence.

Among the crowds walking outside the high steel fences around the conference center was a local resident who had brought her parents and her dog. The resident said that workers had been planting flowers for a year ahead of the summit, and added that a community worker had given her a big package of napkins as a gift after she agreed not to drive her car in the coming week. Shenzhen has halved fares this week on its extensive subway system to help clear the roads for summit buses.

At the same time, however, residents are aware of occasional hassles. “Many friends who live around here plan to travel outside the city because of the inconvenience of transportation — many roads will be closed to traffic,” said the resident, who gave only her family name, Zhang. As is common at high-profile and high-security events in China, she was wary of giving her full name, which can be risky for Chinese citizens.

Few dare to express any misgivings, however, accepting the disruption with resignation. “Even if we’re unhappy about it, we can’t really say so,” said a resident surnamed Wang. “The government wouldn’t welcome our dissatisfaction.”

While gatherings of world leaders always prompt extensive security, China’s efforts in Shenzhen are particularly intensive. Surveillance cameras are ubiquitous along streets in China and not noticeably more common this week in Shenzhen, but there are other, unobtrusive signs of the elaborate security effort underway.

Xiangmei Road, located near the main summit venue and lined with some of China’s costliest condominium towers, was much quieter than usual on Monday. A cleaner outside one of the towers who gave only her family name, Li, said that the family employing her has simply left town this week.

Police have been stationed in apartments on upper floors facing the conference center, drones have been prohibited and the remaining residents have received social media messages urging them to support the gathering, Ms. Li said.

“This place is usually very crowded,” she said, “but look how quiet it is now.”

Ruoxin Zhang and Siyi Zhao contributed research.

#99. EU proposes €45bn loan for Ukraine to avert cash crisis and boost defences

Von der Leyen says she wants to give Kyiv ‘strong hand’ in talks and is confident all 27 member states will back idea

The EU executive has proposed a €45bn (£37.5bn) loan for Ukraine to help with a looming cash crisis and reinforce its defences, as Russia’s relentless bombardment continues.

Announcing the plan to lend Kyiv the money in 2027, the European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, said: “We think this is the best way to give Ukraine a strong hand at the negotiating table. And Ukraine only needs to repay once Russia pays for the damage it has caused in Ukraine.”

She expressed confidence all 27 member states would stand behind the loan, despite four central and eastern European countries having already ruled themselves out.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said he hoped for the swift adoption of the loan and was counting on the support of all EU member states: “Strong support for Ukraine is the best signal to Russia that this war must end,” he wrote on X.

Zelenskyy convinced EU leaders last month that his country faced a €70bn funding gap in 2027. The EU pledged to cover €45bn and wants Ukraine’s other allies to cover the rest.

Only 11 months ago, the EU agreed a €90bn loan for Ukraine, which was intended to cover two-thirds of Kyiv’s military and civilian needs in 2026 and 2027. But as Russia’s attacks have escalated, the cost of Ukraine’s defence and the toll of economic disruption continue to rise.

Andrius Kubilius, the EU defence commissioner, said Ukraine’s military spending was increasingly dominated by drones. Ukraine, he told reporters, expected Russia to use 10m drones this year and 20m in 2027. “Ukraine with EU assistance needs to match – and will match,” he said.

The EU loan is split into two parts: €25bn for weapons and €20bn for civilian needs, including funding essential services, such as schools and hospitals.

Von der Leyen expressed confidence that all member states would support the loan because “all 27 member states say very clearly that they want to support Ukraine”. She said member states could choose to support Ukraine’s civilian needs, rather than military spending.

Slovakia, Czechia, Hungary and Bulgaria, however, have said they do not intend to take part in the latest funding round for Ukraine, prompting a warning from the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, that he would block the EU’s seven-year budget unless all countries got onboard.

The four central and eastern countries are net beneficiaries of the EU budget, while Germany is the largest payer and Ukraine’s biggest EU donor.

The EU wants Ukraine’s non-EU allies – such as Norway, the UK and Canada – to find €25bn for Kyiv in 2027.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The EU proposal included provisions “to help other countries to help us”, a senior commission official said. Brussels has proposed that G7 countries that provide new loans get guarantees that the borrowing can be repaid with future profits from Russia’s frozen assets.

The profits from the Russian assets are being used to repay a €45bn loan issued by the G7 to Ukraine in 2024.

Countries with a triple-A credit rating could opt to provide guarantees to the EU budget up to €5bn to expand European borrowing for Ukraine on capital markets. The commission has identified six countries that fall into this category: Australia, Canada, Liechtenstein, Norway, Singapore and Switzerland.

EU officials previously said the UK and other allies needed to do more to support Ukraine in 2027, arguing that the EU was shouldering too much of the burden. The UK subsequently pledged a £10bn economic and military support package for Ukraine.

Zelenskyy said: “The UK, Canada, Japan, and Norway have been helping tremendously in our defence of life, and establishing clear rules under which they can provide further aid in conjunction with the EU will help even more.”

#98. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#95. Spanish court investigates Julio Iglesias over sexual abuse claims

Case involving allegations from two women employed at singer’s properties in Caribbean had been shelved

A Spanish court is investigating the singer Julio Iglesias over complaints that he sexually abused and physically assaulted two female employees who worked at his properties in the Caribbean.

The announcement of the investigation by the Audiencia Nacional comes almost 11 months after Spanish prosecutors shelved the women’s complaints, arguing the country’s courts had no jurisdiction as the alleged offences took place outside Spain.

But the human rights NGO Women’s Link Worldwide, which is representing the women, has successfully argued that the Audiencia Nacional has jurisdiction over the case and Iglesias’s Spanish nationality provides sufficient grounds to try the case in Spain. It filed a new criminal complaint on the women’s behalf on 3 September.

The allegations emerged in January this year after a three-year joint investigation by the Spanish news site elDiario.es and the Spanish-language TV network Univision Noticias, which gathered testimony from 15 former employees who worked for the now 83-year-old singer between the late 1990s and 2023.

The two complainants – a domestic worker and a physical therapist who were employed at Iglesias’s mansions in the Dominican Republic and the Bahamas – allege the veteran entertainer subjected them “to inappropriate touching, insults and humiliation … in an atmosphere of control and constant harassment”. The offences are alleged to have occurred in 2021.

In a statement released on Wednesday, the Audiencia Nacional said it was investigating the women’s complaint, which accuses Iglesias of “offences against sexual freedom, as well as offences involving bodily harm and degrading treatment”.

The investigating judge, Antonio Piña, said preliminary evidence offered by one of the complainants was sufficient to launch an investigation.

“A photograph of the complainant shows a bruise on one of her arms, consistent with her having been gripped forcefully,” said Piña. “In the same photograph, two marks are visible on one of her thighs – marks that could correspond to a human bite – which the complaint states are the result of her being forced to participate in sexual acts.”

The judge said the “detailed” account submitted, taken with the preliminary evidence provided, was “sufficient to initiate proceedings, without this implying any evaluative judgment regarding the complainant’s credibility”.

However, Piña rejected the women’s allegations of human trafficking and labour offences, and also noted that some of the alleged offences may have expired under the statute of limitations. The judge has called the two women to give evidence in person on 3 February next year.

Iglesias, who has sold more than 300m records during his six-decade career, has denied all the allegations and said he would defend himself against what he described as “a serious affront”.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

In a statement posted on Instagram in January, Iglesias wrote: “With great regret, I am responding to the allegations from two people who used to work in my house. I have never abused, coerced or disrespected any woman. These accusations are absolutely false and pain me deeply.”

Women’s Link said the case showed the need for states to ensure access to justice.

“Everyone has the right to have justice systems investigate facts when there are indications of alleged human rights violations,” said Jovana Ríos Cisneros, the NGO’s executive director. “The Spanish state has an obligation to guarantee equal access to justice, regardless of who the accused person is.”

Information and support for anyone affected by rape or sexual abuse issues is available from the following organisations. In the UK, Rape Crisis offers support on 0808 500 2222 in England and Wales, 0808 801 0302 in Scotland, or 0800 0246 991 in Northern Ireland. In the US, Rainn offers support on 800-656-4673. In Australia, support is available at 1800Respect (1800 737 732). Other international helplines can be found at ibiblio.org/rcip/internl.html

#94. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#92. ‘Devastating consequences’: One Nation plan to slash Australia’s refugee program denounced

Amid widespread condemnation of the proposal, Pauline Hanson also wants to scrap existing parent and aged parent visa program

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

One Nation has vowed to cut Australia’s refugee intake by 90% and scrap the permanent parent visa program under its latest hardline plan to slash migration.

Pauline Hanson announced the policy on Wednesday, labelling it a “tough” approach to migration. It was quickly criticised by political opponents and advocacy groups who labelled the announcement a “predictable and cheap” attack on refugees.

The rightwing party said if it held power it would cut the refugee program from 20,000 places a year to 2,000 and claimed that Australia would still remain above the OECD average for places per capita.

“The cost [to] … the taxpayer is enormous,” Hanson told reporters. “The cost is, on average, about $500,000 a year in a lifetime of expenditure per person in the country.

“We’ve got to take a tough stance on this. My concern is for these people out there … that are doing it tough. You don’t bring more people into the country if they cannot fund their own bed or hospital beds.”

Other nations, including the US and Canada, have tightened their migration programs, with Canada reducing its parent visa program.

Hanson said One Nation was not barring migrant parents from moving to Australia but they would have to be sponsored or they could visit their families on temporary visas.

“We’re not saying parents can’t come out … [but] they will actually be sponsored by the families, not the taxpayers.”

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Under One Nation’s proposal, thousands of parents and grandparents of migrants in Australia waiting for a permanent visa would be refunded their $50,000 fee and kicked out of the country.

“One Nation seems to be living in a fantasy world where basic human decency doesn’t exist, and neither does economic logic,” the Labor senator Tammy Tyrrell told parliament.

“Farms and businesses will be left without the workers they need, which means fewer jobs for Australians too.

“Someone has to pay for that hole. It means higher taxes, more debt, and cuts to services. We need migration for our economic prosperity,” she said.

There are about 150,000 migrant parents in the queue for a visa, with some already on bridging visas.

The home affairs minister, Tony Burke, warned skilled workers would not fill critical jobs in hospitals and nursing homes if they could not bring their families with them.

“What One Nation is proposing is a complete attack on the services that Australians rely on,” Burke said.

Hanson claimed that her plan would save the budget more than $140bn over 10 years by slashing the refugee intake and scrapping permanent parent visas.

Zaki Haidari, who arrived in Australia from Afghanistan as a refugee in 2013 and is now a citizen, said the discussion was retraumatising for former refugees like him.

“Being a refugee is not a choice,” he said. “It’s between death and life.”

Haidari, who now works as a campaigner for Amnesty International Australia, said policies like One Nation’s dashed the hopes of those waiting years in refugee camps for their turn at safety.

“They’re living with that hope that one day the lottery will be for them as well,” he said.

Under One Nation’s policy, migrants from countries on the Albanese government’s “do not travel” list, including Afghanistan, Iran, Lebanon, Syria and Somalia, would still be banned from entering the country.

One Nation says analysis based on Treasury modelling, adjusted for inflation, found the lifetime cost of a humanitarian entrant was $523,000, while accepting the parents of visa holders was estimated to cost the budget $514,000 per person per year.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

Treasury modelling done in December 2021 found the cost was $400,000, with 90% of it borne by the federal government.

Labor has committed to retaining the refugee intake at 20,000 a year while the Coalition has promised to halve it to 10,000.

The foreign affairs minister, Penny Wong, accused One Nation of trying to divide the country. She said Australia had the right balance and questioned Hanson’s claim that Australia would remain above the OECD average under the minor party’s plan.

The OECD reports that Canada and Australia have the most generous refugee settlement programs per capita, but it only uses data for refugees resettled through the UNHCR process.

The intakes of other OECD countries, including Germany and the US, are made up largely of asylum seekers who cross their borders and claim protection, meaning their intake levels appear much lower under the OECD figures Hanson has used.

The opposition leader, Angus Taylor, ruled out further lowering the Coalition’s policy of halving the refugee intake to 10,000, adding “some of the greatest Australians came here as refugees to this country”.

The Greens leader, David Shoebridge, accused One Nation and the two major parties of being “engaged in a race to the bottom to turn back refugees, lock them up in offshore detention, and blame them for the cost-of-living crisis”.

“Pauline Hanson’s attack on refugees is just the latest shameful chapter in a decades-long campaign against people seeking safe shelter,” Shoebridge said.

Meanwhile, the Asylum Seeker Resource Centre called it a “predictable and cheap attack” on refugees, and said Victorians would reject One Nation at the upcoming state election.

The ASRC’s deputy chief executive, Jana Favero, said the public wanted leaders to bring communities together, not divide them.

“People are sick of politicians finger-pointing and blaming our newest arrivals for problems,” Favero said. “Reducing the humanitarian intake will not make Victorians richer or safer. People want solutions, not scapegoats.”

The Refugee Council of Australia noted the humanitarian program only accounted for about 6.6% of net overseas migration.

“Cutting it would do little to affect migration levels but would have devastating consequences for people fleeing war, persecution and violence,” the council’s co-chief executive, Adama Kamara, said.

“Many refugees in our region already have few opportunities to find safety and face the constant risk of being returned to countries where their lives and freedom are at risk.”

Additional reporting by Australian Associated Press

#91. Bikie ‘not surprised’ if he used fake $20,000 souvlaki invoice to pocket thousands on Big Build site, inquiry hears

Royal commission sees text message where Bandidos member Joel Leavitt says ‘just make up a quote that hits 20K’ while working on site for CFMEU

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A Bandidos gang member says it is possible he used a fake $20,000 invoice for souvlaki to pocket thousands of dollars while employed on a Big Build site.

The former CFMEU member and bikie Joel Leavitt faced a second day of examination by the royal commission into alleged corruption across Victoria’s construction sector on Wednesday.

In 2023, while employed as site health and safety representatives, Leavitt and another man, Charles Farrugia, exchanged messages which were shown to the commission.

“Just make up a quote that hits 20K and we will get the other nine off the subbies,” Leavitt wrote. “Which will mean five each.”

The commission viewed a genuine receipt for roughly $9,000 for souvlaki catering, and another allegedly fake invoice for $20,000 which was sent by Leavitt to the site superintendent, Tim Alford, from the firm Acciona.

The commissioner, Chris Kourakis AC, said the evidence strongly suggested the two CFMEU health and safety representatives had organised a meal for workers to get a few souvlaki and had pocketed $5,000 each.

“I’ll accept that,” Leavitt said. “I’m not surprised that I could have.”

The commission also viewed messages between Leavitt and Alford in which the bikie appeared to direct who could and who couldn’t be hired to work on the site.

In one message, Alford appeared to address the arrangement, saying: “I don’t care bringing your mates on and putting them in spotting roles and finding homes. But they all need to do there jobs.”

The counsel assisting the commission Chris Carr SC asked Leavitt if he, on behalf of the CFMEU, was dictating who could work on the site, to which he replied he couldn’t recall.

“I had my own demons at the time, so I may have dropped the ball on a few things,” Leavitt said. “That’s my answer to that question, counsellor.”

Earlier on Wednesday, questioning returned to a $165,000 invoice paid to Leavitt’s now defunct company, JVL Consulting, by the union-aligned subcontractor BK Labour Hire in July 2025.

When shown a copy of the invoice Leavitt was unable to recall anything about it, or even give a clear answer as to what services his former company had provided.

Later on Wednesday, the BK Labour Hire director Bernard Kearney admitted he paid the invoice despite Leavitt not having done any work for the firm.

“I just decided to pay it and move on,” he said.

Carr said there were likely only a couple of reasons why BK Labour would have paid Leavitt, including a fear of violence or to suppress some information he had on them.

“Was it one or both of those two?” Carr asked.

BK Labour also received a succession of invoices from the senior CFMEU organiser Joe Myles totalling $256,000 over about a year.

Carr had earlier asked why Leavitt appeared to become uncomfortable when asked about the invoice.

Leavitt blamed his poor memory on having ADHD, as well as PTSD from being shot in May 2023.

“Around that time I actually got told by gang squad that there was a contract on my life,” he said. “So it brought back some uncomfortable memories.

“That’s why I blocked out a lot of that time.”

Kourakis warned Leavitt it was an offence to fail to answer questions put to him by the commission, something he said could ultimately be tested in a courtroom.

“I’ve been shot … I’ve had drug issues … I am answering these to the best of my recollection,” Leavitt replied.

“How do you think you would act if you’d ever been shot?”

After the commission briefly adjourned for Leavitt to speak to his lawyers, the bikie returned to the stand and said JVL Consulting had provided safety advice to companies. “Maybe helping them out with their procedures and policies and stuff like that,” he said.

The commission viewed a copy of Leavitt’s bank statement at the time which fluctuated between $0 and minus $20, before the $165,000 landed in his account.

“Everyone likes money – I’m sure all youse are on good money for this,” said Leavitt, addressing the bar table.

Kourakis informed Leavitt that the commission had evidence he did not do any work for BK Labour.

He said an inference that could be made was that Leavitt was “standing over” BK Labour for the payment because he needed money.

“If that’s the way you want to look at it, commissioner,” Leavitt replied.

Carr asked if Leavitt knew something about BK Labour that the company would have wanted to keep quiet. “Is the conclusion the commission should draw is that you just stood over them with some sort of explicit or implied threat of violence?” he asked.

Leavitt replied: “I’d like to talk to my counsel before I answer that.”

#87. ‘I didn’t notice his body being purple’: Byron Bay doula left newborn to inflate a jumping castle, court hears

Inquest into deaths of four babies born in free births or at home in northern NSW between 2022 and 2024 hears ‘something was seriously wrong’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Baby DH was born in an inflatable pool, in a candle-lit room in his parents’ home near Byron Bay, with no one medically trained present. There are two very different versions of what happened next.

In the parents’ version, the doula reassured them Baby DH was OK, then left to inflate a jumping castle while their newborn turned purple, became floppy, and struggled to breathe. In the doula’s version, everything seemed OK but she suggested they seek more help if they wanted to.

The New South Wales coroner’s court heard the two competing narratives on Wednesday as part of an ongoing inquest into the deaths of four babies born in free births or home births.

The babies died in NSW’s northern rivers region between 2022 and 2024.

“The evidence will be that from his first minutes of life, [Baby DH] showed signs that something was seriously wrong,” the Lismore courtroom heard.

“His parents saw those signs. They raised them again and again with their doula, who they had engaged to watch for exactly that. Their doula, in this case, was a woman named Amy Lou Smith.

“Each time the parents raised their concerns, they were reassured by Amy Lou. An ambulance was not called until moments before [Baby DH] stopped breathing.”

His father described the baby as floppy after birth. “His colour, his whole body was purple with his hands and feet darker still,” the court heard, and Smith told them some purple in the hands and feet was normal.

“I didn’t notice his body being purple,” Smith told the court.

Told the baby’s colour wasn’t improving, Smith said “as long as his abdomen isn’t purple”, according to evidence from the parents. The mother was shocked. His whole body had been purple all along. His temperature was low, 34.5C. Smith suggested trying to get it up to 37C.

“I believed if they had real concerns, that they would call an ambulance and not be texting a non medically trained doula,” Smith told the court.

Smith, the inquest heard, described his laboured breathing as “cute little noises”. “I didn’t say that,” Smith told the court.

The court heard Smith told the couple Baby DH would have been put on oxygen if he was in hospital. But Smith later told the court she didn’t know what the hospital would have done.

He had strange lumps on his body, and wasn’t feeding. Smith suggested they get the lumps checked out, and warned that babies sometimes take a while to latch on to the breast, according to the parents’ evidence.

The court heard Smith left to set up a jumping castle for her son’s birthday. She sent the couple a picture of the castle later, as they were trying to contact a doctor, the court heard.

Smith told the court the castle was already set up, and the couple was happy for her to leave.

“Everything felt like it was OK,” she said.

Eventually, the parents spoke to the GP they had tried to contact earlier. The GP told them to call triple zero. They did, and did CPR until the paramedics got there. But Baby DH was declared dead at the hospital.

After the father had made the emergency call, Smith also told them to call an ambulance, and sent them instructions on infant CPR.

Throughout the hearing, Smith denied that any of her advice or comments constituted clinical advice.

Neonatologist Dr Paul Evans, the court heard, had found Baby DH had a complex heart defect, one that would have shown up on an X-ray. The baby could have been treated, and a birth plan with proximity to a major hospital and specialist cardiac care prepared, the court heard.

The child’s death was not inevitable, Evans found.

While pregnant, Baby DH’s mother decided to rely on a doula. She first approached a woman the court heard was likely to be Sita Tara Kali, a self-described birth keeper who was also involved in the care of another baby previously mentioned during the inquest. But Kali didn’t feel she could help, so they turned to Smith.

The court heard the couple paid for an online “sovereign birth course” for Smith and was reassured that she had enough experience. They agreed on an emergency plan.

Smith, who became a birth doula in 2020, was quizzed on her experience in court.

She has a degree in fine art and has worked as a social worker and a youth worker.

She told the court she is not medically trained in any way but did an online “full-spectrum doula course”. She learned CPR but was not familiar with how to apply it to a newborn – she denied she had given the parents the impression she was.

She had no training about a newborn’s colour, breathing, or feeding, but she did go to a retreat where she learned about referring a woman to more support if needed.

She had been present at 15 births over four years, nine alongside a midwife, and conceded she didn’t know what a low-risk birth would look like. She had read a couple of books about birthing, but they were more “spiritual”, the court heard.

She agreed that she now believes the best model of care for a home birth is for a clinically trained midwife to work alongside a doula.
