#73. American soldier arrested by UK police on suspicion of spying taken into US military custody

Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested on 10 November

A US Army officer arrested in the UK on suspicion of spying for Russia earlier this month has been taken into US military custody, British counter-terror police have said.

The serviceman has been identified as Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner, BBC News understands. The 41-year-old - who served in the US Army since 2008 - was arrested near Gloucester last week by British counter-terror police.

Tyner was based at Imjin Barracks in Innsworth, Gloucestershire, the headquarters of Nato's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps.

Counter Terrorism Policing said he is now in US custody over suspected national security and espionage allegations. At the time of his arrest, Russia said it had "nothing to do with it".

British police previously said he was being held in London after he was arrested under section one and section three of the National Security Act 2023, which relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The day after Tyner's arrest on 10 November, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said he was "unaware" of what it was connected to.

"We have nothing to do with it, and it has nothing to do with us," he told reporters in Moscow.

The US serviceman is understood to have been based at Imjin Barracks in Gloucestershire

On Tuesday, Assistant Commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of Counter Terrorism Policing, said: "Subsequently, a decision was made to pass the investigation to them [their American partners] to progress."

"These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place," he added.

Tyner's arrest was the result of a long-running operation by MI5 - the UK's domestic intelligence and security agency - in collaboration with the FBI, the BBC understands.

He had previously served in Afghanistan and Iraq, but at the time of his arrest was working with NATO's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps near Gloucester - where he was said to be entrusted with sensitive information which would have been extremely damaging if leaked to Russia.

Detectives sent the Crown Prosecution Service a file on the senior US soldier's alleged spying for consideration - allegations described by the head of Counter Terrorism Policing as "extremely serious."

But at that stage the Attorney General's Office in London got involved. As did the US authorities.

And now the decision has been taken that Lieutenant Colonel Tyner should be taken into custody by the US Military.

It means he will not stand trial in the UK, but under US Uniform Code of Military Justice.

FBI director Kash Patel said an army officer was in custody "as the result of an espionage investigation and the close cooperation" between the bureau, US Army and various UK partners.

"Together we worked to identify a potential threat to national security and take swift action," he said.

"This FBI has zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk."

US Brig Gen Sean Stinchon said the case should serve as a "stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath" they all take.

"Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardising our collective defence, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served," he added.

#71. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#69. Hi-tech Shenzhen takes centre stage as Xi sends ‘Chinamaxxing’ message at Apec summit

Host city offers showcase of China’s rise into a confident and innovative power central to global economy

In a Shenzhen square on a balmy evening, locals and tourists watched the dozens of glinting towers that make up the city’s skyline ripple through the colour spectrum – cyan and violet, cyber green, China’s national scarlet and gold – repeatedly flashing the words “Apec 2026”.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit is a dream showcase for one of China’s most advanced cities, and the country’s leader, Xi Jinping, is basking in its neon glow.

On the sidelines, a series of high-profile bilateral meetings and the announcement of a historic three-way summit with Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin have cemented Xi’s status as an agenda setter on the global stage.

The broader Apec summit, meanwhile, provides an opportunity for China to present itself as a steward of multilateralism against what it terms “unilateralism and protectionism” – that is to say, trade measures taken against Beijing by countries such as the US.

All this is set against the futuristic backdrop that is Shenzhen, a city that perhaps better than no other embodies China’s rise into a confident, technologically innovative power central to the global economy.

A photo display in the Apec media centre charts Shenzhen’s transformation from sparsely populated marshland, calling it “a miracle in the history of world development created by the Chinese people”.

“[Xi] wants people to go home Chinamaxxing,” said Prof Astrid Nordin, of King’s College London. “Shenzhen is itself part of the message. It’s a showcase of Chinese technological and economic achievements, and of China’s claim that it offers opportunities for prosperity in a world increasingly characterised by uncertainty and conflict.”

Like the light show, the summit is being carefully coordinated. The Financial Times cited people familiar with the matter as saying it would be one of the most tightly controlled in the organisation’s history, with leaders having to travel to a state guesthouse beside Shenzhen’s tiger safari park to meet Xi. The paper said diplomats had grumbled that the format “seems designed to reduce the free-flowing discussion characteristic of prior editions”.

Setting out its expectations for the summit, China’s foreign ministry has said it looks forward to “practical results in key areas such as trade, connectivity, digital economy, AI and so on”.

Already, Xi has announced trade wins with Canada’s Mark Carney, extending remission measures for certain Chinese steel and aluminium products by two years in exchange for tariff relief on Canadian pork, fish and seafood exports.

A meeting with Papua New ⁠Guinea’s leader, meanwhile, showed how Beijing’s geopolitical priorities have been absorbed and reflected by its allies, with James Marape recounting to Xi how he had closed Taiwan’s office in his country despite domestic challenges in doing so.

The most important meeting will be between Xi and Trump. The leaders of the world’s two superpowers are expected to tackle a broad range of issues including AI and trade. In a move that could strain the fragile tariff truce that the two leaders first agreed last year, the Trump administration said on Monday it would further restrict China’s access to advanced AI chips.

Christopher Adams, a senior adviser at the law firm Covington who was at the centre of trade negotiations with China for the Obama and first Trump administrations, said: “The trade truce may increasingly look like a ceasefire in a war where major combat operations remain on hold but skirmishes are escalating to the point where the line between peace and war is blurred.

“I don’t see pressure to announce a further extension of the trade truce in Shenzhen. If a significant extension is announced, that would be an upside surprise.”

China has not hesitated to leverage Apec’s multinational platform in service of its arguments. A commentary by the state news agency Xinhua on Tuesday said: “Amid deepening geopolitical rivalries and growing global divisions, [China’s hosting] sends a clear signal that great-power competition need not be a zero-sum game, and that one country’s development need not come at the expense of another.”

The chair of the Apec senior officials’ meeting, Chen Xu, said Beijing had made trade “openness” a key focus of its Apec year and looked forward to “achieving more practical outcomes on the trade front”.

The much anticipated trilateral summit with Trump and Putin, on the other hand, could prove largely symbolic. For Xi, “it provides the appearance of China shaping the world order as an equal to the United States,” said Dr Kevork Oskanian, an expert on Russia at the University of Exeter. “Agreement on substantial issues, which would, in any case, require extensive preparatory negotiations, is unlikely.”

For China’s ruling Communist party, the political pomp has another key purpose. Nordin said summits were “occasions for leaders to tell stories about the world, about their country’s place in it … and one of the most important audiences for those stories is at home.”

For the locals watching the skyscraper light show on Monday night, the symbolism was not lost. China’s export might had helped it become increasingly powerful on the world stage, said one local man, who did not give his name. “It’s so great that the summit will take place in Shenzhen,” he said. “I feel very proud.”

#68. US loses measles elimination status after 26 years amid record case numbers

BBC's Madeline Halpert explains why the US lost its measles elimination status and what it could mean.

After more than two decades, the US has lost its measles-free status, following two years of record case numbers and deaths.

The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a specialised service of the United Nations, determined the US has had uninterrupted transmission of the same strain of measles for more than 12 months.

Dr Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, announced that Mexico and Bolivia have also lost their measles elimination status, attributing the resurgence in the region to persistent "gaps in vaccination".

The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) said it was committed to "regaining measles elimination status" by expanding access to vaccines.

The US has seen 5,400 cases of measles this year alone, with 10 measles-related deaths, the most the country has seen since the 1990s.

The measles virus - with its signature blotchy red rash - used to infect millions of children a year before a vaccine was invented. The extremely contagious virus can be fatal, and some patients can experience long-term complications including encephalitis and immune amnesia, which resets the immune system, leaving it with only limited ability to respond to new infections.

The US recommends two measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) shots for children between ages one and six, which is 97% effective in protecting against the illness.

PAHO, a group within the World Health Organization, revoked the US's measles-elimination after determining that the virus had circulated nonstop for more than a year.

"These figures are a serious warning for our region," PAHO's director Barbosa said at a news conference on Tuesday. "Measles is resurging globally amid persistent gaps in vaccination."

He added that Canada, which lost its status of having eliminated measles last year after seeing several large outbreaks, has made strides this year to interrupt transmission.

Daniel Salas, the executive manager of immunisations at PAHO, said that the US and others who lost elimination status would be required to submit corrective action plans outlining how they would attempt to improve vaccination rates.

US Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr Kennedy has attempted to make a series of sweeping changes to US vaccine recommendations and policies. The vaccine sceptic has also frequently cast doubt on the safety of MMR shot, as vaccination rates have declined across the US in recent years.

Kennedy has argued that the rise in measles cases started before he took the helm at HHS and that neighbouring countries have also seen more infections.

Responding to Tuesday's announcement, the CDC said the agency and its partners were "committed to stopping measles outbreaks, protecting Americans, and regaining measles elimination status".

"Together, we will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to vaccines, and help communities close gaps in protection," the agency said.

CDC Director Erica Schwartz said the measles elimination designation was not what protected Americans.

"Vaccination does," she said. "We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities."

In a post on social media, Kennedy said: "I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles."

"We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," he also said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment."

The UK, Spain and Austria were among European nations that lost their measles-free status earlier this year, along with countries including Russia, Azerbaijan and Uzbekistan.

Nuwan Gunawardhana, an assistant professor of medicine with Columbia University Irving Medical Center, said the US losing its elimination status was "a huge leap backwards".

The US was the first country to eliminate measles in the Americas thanks to vaccination campaigns, noted William Moss, a paediatric infectious disease specialist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health.

Dr Gunawardhana said it took massive efforts from public health officials and clinicians to build trust with patients to increase uptake of the MMR vaccine, leading to decades in the US with few infections and deaths.

But that trust has been steadily eroding since the Covid-19 pandemic, when Dr Gunawardhana said patients were confronted with misinformation about the safety of vaccines and lost even more trust in public health systems.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity, which limits the spread of measles and protects those who cannot get vaccinated, including newborns.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity - which limits the spread of measles

An outbreak in Pennsylvania has infected over 1,400 people - likely a large underestimate - and killed seven people, according to state officials.

Kennedy, the health secretary, has sparred with Pennsylvania officials over how it is counting the measles deaths, with CDC declining for now to record some of the deaths in its database.

Last year, the health secretary fired all the members of a top vaccine advisory panel and replaced them with his own appointees, some of whom were vaccine critics.

The panel recommended a number of vaccine changes - including no longer requiring the Hepatitis B vaccine for all newborns - but those recommendations were blocked by a federal judge.

President Donald Trump this year also cast doubt on the safety of the MMR vaccine, issuing an executive order for the shot to be split into three separate immunisations for measles, mumps and rubella, a move public health experts say is costly and could sow confusion about an already safe vaccine.

Doctors and public health officials say the misinformation from Kennedy and Trump has played a major role in falling vaccination rates.

"It's confusing patients. It's confusing families," Dr Gunawardhana said. "We need a clear message from the top."

The federal government's vaccine manoeuvres have left public health officials and states to fill in the gaps, offering their own separate recommendations upholding decades-long practices.

#66. Jensen Huang to testify before Congress on ‘potential breach of national security’

High-stakes hearing comes in wake of resignation from Nvidia after alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia’s former CEO Jensen Huang will testify before several congressional committees on 1 December, setting the stage for a high-stakes hearing in the wake of his resignation following an affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

Tim Teter, Nvidia’s general counsel, has also agreed to appear for a closed-door transcribed interview the following day, 2 December.

“We look forward to hearing from Jensen Huang and Tim Teter on their roles in a potential breach of our national security,” Congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar and Rick Crawford, who chair the committees, said in a press release. “The American people need answers on Mr Huang and Nvidia’s role in allowing dangerous [People’s Republic of China] actors to access our most advanced chips, as well as the role of a potential Chinese intelligence asset in this scandal.”

The three congressmen chair the foreign affairs committee, the select committee on the Chinese Communist party, and the intelligence committee of the House of Representatives, respectively. On 12 November, they had signed a letter warning Huang that he could face a subpoena if he did not agree to the closed-door meeting.

Nvidia chips are key to powering the artificial intelligence boom. The Trump administration has prohibited the selling of advanced artificial intelligence chips to China.

Huang stepped down as Nvidia’s CEO after allegations of extramarital relationships with the suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang and two Nvidia employees surfaced.

Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, had been Megaspeed International’s executive director. The Singapore-based cloud computing company has bought nearly $5bn in AI chips and advanced hardware. The company said Alice Huang stepped down in 2025.

Megaspeed ultimately facilitated about $12bn in chip sales, in violation of US export controls. On Friday, Malaysia stated that they were investigating whether the company diverted advanced AI ships to other countries.

A recent joint Department of Commerce and Department of Justice letter, stated that the Department of Commerce, under its secretary, Howard Lutnick, first started an investigation in Megaspeed on 2024 for potential export control violations. The justice department, led by the attorney general, Todd Blanche, took over in 2025.

In his first remarks since his resignation, Jensen Huang said on Thursday he would be willing to return to Nvidia as CEO. Elon Musk and other Silicon Valley tech giants, along with 165,000 signatories to an open letter, have called for his reinstatement. Trump has also signaled his openness for the disgraced tech CEO’s return.

#63. Putin's Crimean Bridge partly collapses as Zelensky hails 'unprecedented' attack by Ukraine

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimea bridge

Ukraine has severely damaged a critical bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea, in what President Volodymyr Zelensky says was an "unprecedented operation" weeks in the making.

Images shared by Ukrainian officials show a section of the Crimean Bridge collapsed into the water, dealing a logistical and symbolic blow to Moscow's war effort.

The Kremlin said it would rebuild the bridge "better than before".

Meanwhile in Kyiv, Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko said traffic had partially resumed on the Pivnichnyi (Northern) and Pivdennyi (Southern) bridges - key crossings over the Dnipro River - after they had been closed following Russian strikes overnight.

Ukraine has long targeted the Crimean Bridge, which provides a road and rail route for Russian soldiers heading towards the front line, but has struggled to close it for good.

While Kyiv has been trying to increase the cost to Russia of waging war economically, by hitting oil refineries and depots, striking the bridge is an attempt to increase the political cost on Russian President Vladimir Putin.

The timing of the strike comes at a significant moment, ahead of Putin meeting the leaders of China and the US. This could cloud his moment back in the spotlight.

The Crimean Bridge was built by Russia after it invaded and illegally annexed Ukraine's southern peninsula in 2014.

The $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) piece of infrastructure is widely seen in Russia and Ukraine as Putin's personal pet project: His close tycoon friend Arkady Rotenberg built it, and then Putin personally opened it by driving a lorry over it in May 2018.

Putin (centre) personally opened the Crimean Bridge by driving a lorry over it in May 2018

In Ukraine, the much-hated bridge is seen as one of the main symbols of Russia's continuing occupation of Ukrainian lands.

"We're drinking champagne!" Andrii, an engineer who lives in Kyiv, told the BBC.

He said the latest attack would "deal a very heavy blow to the morale of the Russians".

Some Ukrainians are concerned that Putin might now respond by carrying out even more attacks against civilian infrastructure, but Andrii said: "I don't think the Russians can make things much worse; they're already pounding us every single night with everything they've got."

Images show the damage to the Crimean Bridge from the overnight attack

The damage after the overnight attack, involving cruise missiles and drones, appeared to be far more severe than during previous strikes on the bridge, with a large arched section apparently destroyed.

Video footage appeared to show critical damage to the road section, with remnants of the support arch and road deck seen protruding from the water below.

Zelensky said the strike was the result of a seven-stage operation by the military and security services, which saw Ukrainian forces "gradually destroy air defence systems, radars, electronic warfare equipment, and other designated targets on the way to the final objective".

He said several types of Ukrainian drones as well as the country's Flamingo and Neptune cruise missiles were fired overnight during a "pivotal stage".

The Ukrainian president also said the rail section parallel to the bridge's road had also been hit by a wave of drones, though images showed its supporting arch was still standing and Russia's transport ministry later said some rail services resumed.

Ukraine's state security service SBU said its operations had "systematically suppressed enemy air defence systems" over a month, and said it showed "no Russian defence systems can guarantee the safety of illegal objects occupied by the invaders".

Meanwhile, Ukraine's military said the roadway on the bridge is "out of action", with no vehicle able to cross it, and the railway section is still standing, but with a damaged arch.

The Kremlin said Putin had been briefed on the overnight attack. Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said the bridge would "be restored following Ukrainian strikes, and it will be even better than before", and did not rule out the possibility the strikes were linked to Putin's visit to China.

Russia's Investigative Committee confirmed structural damage and said it had opened a "criminal case... on terrorism charges" after attacks by "Ukraine's armed formations".

For the second day in a row, Russia recorded the biggest Ukrainian drone attack since the war began, with 2,256 drones downed, according to the defence ministry.

Zelensky had warned of major attacks in the days leading up to a meeting between Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping in China's Shenzhen on Thursday.

Watch: BBC reports from Kyiv bridge hit by Russian attack

In Kyiv, loud explosions were heard just before midnight on Monday, with Klitschko saying shortly after that the city had suffered "damage to critical infrastructure systems" and disruption to heating and water supplies.

On Tuesday, Zelensky said four ballistic missiles had targeted the Southern Bridge, and another three had been fired on the Northern Bridge.

Some of Kyiv's above-ground metro services were also damaged in the overnight attacks, local officials said, after civilians were told to go to civil defence shelters.

Eight major bridges span the Dnipro River, connecting the western and eastern parts of Kyiv, and an estimated three million residents rely on the bridges to travel across the city.

Elsewhere, three people have been injured in a Russian missile attack in the central Cherkasy region, according to local officials.

The long-term impact of the Crimean Bridge strike is still not fully clear, though the risk of a serious transport and logistics crisis for the Russian military has increased significantly.

The collapse of the arched section has cut off the peninsula's direct road and rail links with Russia â at least until the crossing can be repaired.

However, there is still a land corridor running through the occupied parts of Ukraine's southern Zaporizhzhia and Kherson regions - though Kyiv has been systematically striking transport routes across northern Crimea, as well as bridges and other crossings in the area around Chongar.

This has already caused problems with the delivery of fuel and other supplies, including military cargo.

There is a ferry crossing across the Kerch Strait, which connects the Black Sea with the Sea of Azov. It could partly compensate for the loss of the bridge, but its capacity is very limited and it is also vulnerable to attack.

Russia could also supply Crimea by sea, but this route is also risky, as Ukrainian naval and aerial drones operate extensively in the area.

In addition, debris from the Crimean Bridge â particularly its enormous arch â may have blocked the shipping channel.

This would make it harder for larger vessels to pass through and create further problems for the Russian-occupied ports in the Sea of Azov, including Mariupol and Berdyansk.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022. Approximately one-fifth of Ukraine is now under Russian occupation and Kyiv has refused Putin's demand to give up areas of Ukraine's eastern Donbas that remain under its control.

#60. South Korea’s high-stakes university exams upended by AI cheating scandal

South Korea has been gripped by revelations of AI-aided cheating, just days before more than half a million students are to sit high-stakes entrance exams

The integrity of South Korea’s career-defining university entrance exam has been thrown into doubt after the top scorer in a recent professional exam admitted using a concealed camera and AI to cheat.

On Thursday, more than half a million people, mostly school students, are due to sit the exam, known as the suneung, at 1,312 centres across the country. The exam runs for a gruelling eight hours over a single day and determines which university a candidate can attend and, in turn, their future prospects.

The country goes to great lengths to ensure the smooth running of the suneung. Aircraft take-offs and landings are rescheduled during the 25-minute English listening paper to minimise noise, while artillery fire and tank movements are suspended.

The stock market opens an hour late and cars are stopped 200 metres from every centre. Extra trains run from 6am, police drive latecomers to their exam halls, and tourist attractions open late to ease traffic. Offices are asked to open at 10am.

But in recent days, exam organisers have faced a renewed threat to the validity of the entire exercise, after the top scorer in this year’s tax accountancy exam- who got just one question wrong out of 160– admitted on Sunday he had cheated by using AI.

Prime minister Han Seong-sook told a cabinet meeting in Seoul on Tuesday that AI-assisted cheating was a “social evil” that the government would “root out”.

“We will block outright the carrying of smart devices during the exam,” she said. The education ministry and other agencies should approach it “as if staking their jobs on it”, she added.

Han said smart devices must be turned off and handed in, while bags left in corridors would be checked for any devices that were still on. Exam officials would also inspect all candidates’ glasses and watches, as well as their collars, sleeves and buttons for concealed cameras.

The government will also send emergency alerts to every phone in the country, which sound at full volume and cannot be silenced, in the hope of catching out anyone who has not handed in their device.

More invigilators will be deployed, and the government said it was preparing to switch off school wifi inside exam centres and public wifi nearby, and that personal hotspots, which cannot be blocked, would be traced through connection records.

The weekend’s cheating revelation captured national headlines in South Korea, stoking concern about the security of the suneung.

In a video posted to his lawyer’s YouTube channel, the exam cheat said he had photographed the questions with a concealed microcamera, sent them to an AI system and read the answers from a smartphone. “I cheated in the tax accountant exam,” he said. “My top placing is not the result of legitimate effort.”

His lawyer handed police a camera, a laptop and a phone. No charges have been brought, and the prime minister has ordered procedures to begin that would void his result and bar him for five years.

The education minister, Choi Kyo-jin, told parliament on Monday that while authorities could screen out the smart glasses already on sale at Thursday’s exam, newer variants could not be dealt with perfectly.

Choi has ruled out postponing the exam, which has been delayed several times before, including in 2017, after an earthquake struck the south-eastern city of Pohang, and in 2020 due to the Covid pandemic.

Smart glasses have been banned in exams since the end of June. In October, several candidates were caught with them while sitting university-run admissions exams. At the suneung, wearing a pair counts as using one even when switched off.

The education ministry looked at using radio-frequency equipment to detect the glasses in exam halls, but concluded conditions varied too much between venues for deployment this year. Checks will instead rely on visual inspection, with at least one dedicated official placed in each exam centre.

Teachers’ unions have criticised the government response as belated. A survey last month of 2,716 secondary school teachers by the Korean Teachers and Education Workers Union showed that 82.3% said they could not tell whether a candidate was wearing smart glasses under the current procedure, with only 3.5% saying they could.

To avoid facing scrutiny on exam day, some candidates are reportedly switching to contact lenses. An optician in Suwon, south of Seoul, said students who had never worn contacts were coming in to ask how to put them in.

Han said on X on Monday that the checks would be handled carefully “so that candidates acting in good faith do not suffer unnecessary anxiety or inconvenience”.

In his video confession, the accountancy test cheat addressed suneung candidates directly. “The suneung is coming up soon,” he said.

“To anyone thinking of cheating as I did: I am going to tell the investigators everything, so please don’t think, ‘I won’t get caught.’”

→ Baca artikel asli

#59. Irishman charged with destroying US military aircraft with axe

The military plane, seen above, was effectively destroyed by the vandalism

US officials have charged an Irishman with destroying a $60m (Â£45m) American military aircraft after he allegedly sneaked onto the tarmac of an airport in Ireland.

Daithi O'Corrain, 48, is accused of doing extensive research before he allegedly used chemicals and various tools - including an axe - to damage a US Air National Guard C-130 Hercules parked at Shannon Airport near Limerick.

American prosecutors have charged him with destruction of government property, which carries a maximum sentence of 10 years in prison.

O'Corrain is not currently in US custody, according to the Associated Press news agency, and it is unclear whether he has a lawyer who could speak on his behalf.

In filings unsealed by prosecutors in New York on Tuesday, agents from the FBI argued that the act jeopardised US national security. They say the plane was parked at the airport while en route to another country on a US military assignment.

"As alleged, Daithi O'Corrain carried out a deliberate, planned attack against a US military aircraft, causing tens of millions of dollars in damage and jeopardizing critical military operations," prosecutors said in a court filing.

"This was not an act of spontaneous vandalism," said FBI Assistant Director in Charge James Barnacle Jr, explaining O'Corrain "spent months researching the aircraft, acquiring tools and materials, and preparing to maximize the destruction he could inflict."

Officials say that the suspect planned extensively for the 11 April 2026 raid, and brought tools with him for the demolition, which took place over several hours.

He entered the plane by breaking a locking mechanism, officials say, then used straps to close the doors before covering the windows.

"O'Corrain then proceeded to inflict severe damage to the interior of the Aircraft by, among other things, cutting wire bundles and hydraulic lines within the Aircraft, crimping a liquid oxygen line within the Aircraft, destroying every gauge in the Aircraft's cockpit, and puncturing the Aircraft's air ducts," the US indictment says.

He also used gallium, a substance that can erode aluminum and steel, ball bearings which he poured in the fuel tank, and an axe which he used to puncture the exterior of the plane.

He was caught by airport crew, and filmed standing on top of the plane. He was arrested at the scene, and charged by Ireland's national police with causing criminal damages.

O'Corrain seen standing on the rear of the aircraft in a handout from the US Department of Justice

The US charges say that he had planned well, because he avoided cutting a line that would have risked causing massive explosion.

According to the Limerick Post, external, O'Corrain is from Cork City. Supporters have described him as an anti-war activist.

Shannon Airport is a key hub for US military personnel, and is frequently the scene of anti-war protests. It was used by President Donald Trump in September for his visit to Ireland.

US prosecutors note that there have been several breaches there in recent years by protesters, including in May 2025 when three people drove a van through a perimeter fence, and then again later that month when a US airplane was sprayed with paint, making it temporarily inoperable.

#55. Rubio says US not 'under systemic attack' after series of tech outages

Watch: Marco Rubio says recent tech outages are 'completely unrelated' to one another

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has dismissed widespread concerns online that the series of recent tech outages in the past week are connected, telling reporters there was "zero evidence" of any link.

Rubio acknowledged that "people are fearful" the spate of outages, which included all flights being grounded in the US and widespread disruptions to banking, Apple and Microsoft services, could be linked or part of a plot.

"We are not under systemic attack," he said. "These events, one has nothing to do with the other, they are not inter-related."

Cyber-security experts have agreed that there is no evidence linking the outages, and many of the issues appear due to updates to old infrastructure.

In the past week, there has been a massive Microsoft outage, an apparent tech glitch which grounded all US flights for hours, and issues for the National Security Agency, outages affecting the biggest US banks and Apple.

Every affected company or agency has said the issue was not cyber-related and not the work of an outside actor.

Rubio's comments came hours after US Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton also addressed the issue, telling Fox News the outages were not the product of a cyber-attack. He explained "lots of institutions" are patching or upgrading systems, which has caused some disruption.

Rubio was asked whether the Trump administration was concerned public fears could lead to stockpiling or a run on the banks.

He said the best way to combat such fears was for the US government to share everything they know with the American people.

"Every indicator we have is that every single one of these events has an explanation or a cause that has nothing to do with a cyber-criminal, has nothing to do with a hostile actor or another country," he said.

Technology-related disruptions have happened almost daily since Thursday, when a Microsoft outage disrupted many of its services, including email.

The next day, the US Federal Aviation Administration halted flights across the US for more than four hours with a rare national ground stop. It was the longest halting of flights since the 11 September 2001 attacks on the US.

The US transport secretary said the issue was technical and caused by a copper wire upgrade and outdated equipment.

On Monday, a United Airlines technology failure delayed about 150 flights and a JPMorgan Chase software problem disrupted service at the biggest and most utilised banks in the US.

Both United and Chase said a software issue was behind the glitch.

Apple users were impacted by outages affecting iCloud and Apple Pay on Tuesday. The company said the cause was not a cyber-attack but the outage was triggered by an an internal update.

The same day, a highly classified network used by the US National Security Agency (NSA) for internal communications also went offline for a period of time.

Passengers wait to check in at Los Angeles International Airport as flights resume after being grounded for several hours on November 13 due to a major communications outage.

Cybersecurity experts say there is no evidence connecting the Microsoft, airline, and bank disruptions from the last five days.

Kris Beevers, CEO and co-founder of the network and infrastructure management company NetBox Labs, acknowledged the circulating theories on social media linking the barrage of outages.

"There is no conspiracy here," he told the BBC.

"There is something more ordinary, and more important to understand: the infrastructure that runs the world is changing faster than ever, and change is risk. Much of it is 30 to 40 years old, with decades of technical debt, and it's now being asked to change very quickly."

Beevers said AI is only part of the story.

"It's putting more strain on these legacy networks, and it's also creating real urgency around security," he said.

"Our customers are the amazing teams behind these networks at airlines, banking, energy, and retail who are essentially doing heart surgery on a global scale to get these networks back up and running quickly."

Vice President of Threat Intelligence for Palo Alto Networks Unit 42, Sherrod DeGrippo, said: "What this week really underscores is how tightly coupled, and fragile, our core digital infrastructure is, where a single software bug or router failure can cascade nationwide."

#52. Apec summit live: Xi welcomes Trump on China visit as Putin claims Europe in ‘humiliating decline’

Summit in Chinese tech hub Shenzhen set to include highly anticipated meeting between Xi, Trump and Putin

Shenzhen takes centre stage as Xi sends ‘Chinamaxxing’ message at Apec summit

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a separate meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official.

The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

If confirmed, it would be their first bilateral since they met in Alaska in 2025 for talks that failed to reach a deal on the war in Ukraine.

Trump had already suggested he might meet with Putin, as well as with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, but neither option had been added to his official schedule.

Before landing in Shenzhen, Donald Trump enjoyed lunch in Manila with Philippine president Ferdinand Marcos Jr at the Malacañang presidential palace.

Speaking during a meeting ahead of lunch, Marcos revealed that he had arranged for fried chicken from Filipino fast food chain Jollibee to be delivered to the US delegation as a late-night snack last night, praising Trump for “working very hard”.

The gift may have gone down particularly well with Trump, who has something of a reputation for his love of fast food. Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum won praise and went viral on Chinese social media on Wednesday for taking an economy flight to attend a regional summit in Shenzhen.

World leaders this week descended on the southern Chinese city to attend the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meetings – many arriving in specially chartered commercial airliners or dedicated government aircraft like US president Donald Trump’s Air Force One.

Sheinbaum, however, flew in economy class from Mexico City to Shenzhen on a China Southern Airlines flight, media reports said. She arrived in China on Monday.

I have often said that to see a thousand years of China, one should visit Xi’an; to see five hundred years of China, one should visit Beijing; and to see a hundred years of China, one should visit Shanghai. Today, I would like to say to you all: to see fifty years of China, come to Shenzhen.

Vladimir Putin and Donald Trump could have several opportunities to speak during the Apec summit in China, according to Russia’s state news agency, Tass.

Citing Kremlin aide Yuri Ushakov, Tass reported that the two leaders could exchange words informally at various points during the summit.

They will communicate “on their feet, on the steps, in chairs”, Ushakov reportedly said, suggesting their interactions need not be limited to a formal sit-down meeting.

Xi Jinping told Donald Trump he hoped their talks would help advance a “constructive, strategic and stable relationship” between China and the US, based on “respect, fairness and reciprocity”, according to China’s state news agency Xinhua.

double quotation markWhen I visited the United States in September this year, president Trump and his wife made a special trip to the airport to welcome me, and a grand welcoming ceremony was held.

Chinese state media have released a readout of Xi Jinping’s earlier meeting with Vladimir Putin.

He said “the two sides should leverage the mechanisms for exchanges between the two countries’ governments, legislative bodies, militaries and political parties, ensuring that the consensus reached by the two countries’ heads of state is effectively implemented”, according to the readout.

As a reminder, Ukraine carried out a major strike on the Kerch Bridge linking occupied Crimea with Russia, destroying its central road section in an operation involving two strikes over consecutive days.

The attack earlier this week dealt a significant blow to a key transport link and a symbolic project for Vladimir Putin, as the Russian president sat down with Chinese leader Xi Jinping for talks at the Apec summit.

My colleague Jakub Krupa has the latest on the Ukraine conflict over on our Europe live blog here:

While we await Trump to disembark Air Force Once, we have this report from our China correspondent Rebecca Bailey in Shenzhen:

Canada’s Mark Carney has hit back at Vladimir Putin’s earlier assertion that sanctions no longer work in this day and age.

“Mr Putin can ask his financial institutions, he can ask his oligarch friends, he can ask his hockey players whether or not sanctions are a thing of the past. I think he’ll find out that they’re very real, they’re biting,” Carney told reporters.

In his address to the summit this morning, Putin had said that “the traditional tools of economic dominance and de facto coercion have largely run their course, having ⁠lost much of their intimidating power”.

Speaking at an outdoor press conference as purple clouds lingered ominously in the background, Carney said: “Not for the first time, Vladimir Putin is lying about the situation on the ground. Sanctions are very real and they’re going to continue to increase as long as this war goes on.”

Carney had previously said he would not boycott a group photo with Putin out of respect for hosts China. But he said if he were to find himself next to the Russian president, he would make Canada’s position clear.

Carney met China’s president Xi Jinping on Tuesday, with the two countries announcing remission measures for certain Chinese steel and aluminium products by two years in exchange for tariff relief on Canadian pork, fish and seafood exports.

The two men also discussed AI, Carney said Wednesday, adding he had taken the opportunity to share Canada’s views on a model for global governance.

“The absence of any global governance around AI is an enormous risk,” he said. “It’s an enormous economic risk but it’s a biohazard risk, and it’s a risk to democracy, human dignity, other factors... So it is past time to develop global governance frameworks for these sectors.”

Carney is due to meet Donald Trump later today, he said, where they would discuss efforts to reach a trade deal.

“We have been making progress... Two steps forward, one step back type thing,” he said.

He said he fully respected Trump’s engagement with China.

“I’m going to say something positive here about the initiative around denuclearization, the trilateral meeting that is going to happen,” he said. “Guess what? Canada’s in favor of denuclearization. If the relationship can be used towards that end, we’re very much supportive of it.”

Donald Trump has arrived in Shenzhen, China, for the Apec summit, Chinese state media reported.

#49. Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years for Iran-backed plot to kill Trump

A Pakistani man with ties to Iran's government has been sentenced to 27 years in prison for his role in a plot to assassinate US President Donald Trump and other officials.

Asif Merchant was found guilty in March of attempting to commit an act of terrorism transcending national boundaries and murder for hire after trying to hire hitmen to kill Trump, former President Joe Biden, and former US ambassador to the United Nations Nikki Haley.

The hitmen were instead undercover FBI agents.

In court, he said he was acting under the direction of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) to avenge the 2020 US assassination of Qasem Soleimani, Iran's topmost military commander.

New York US federal judge Eric Ross Komitee sentenced Merchant on Tuesday to 330 months (27 years and six months) in prison, months after a jury handed its guilty verdict.

Although other US officials were potential targets, the assassination plot was largely focused on Trump because he was president at the time Soleimani was killed. Trump has spoken publicly about ordering the US airstrike that killed the Iranian general while he was travelling in Iraq.

In response to the killing, Iran's late Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei said "severe revenge awaits the criminals" behind the attack.

Prosecutors said Merchant initially travelled to Iran for religious pilgrimages and he was then introduced to an IRGC handler. He travelled to Iran 15 times where he received training from Iranian intelligence officers, prosecutors said, before flying to the US in April 2024 to carry out the plot.

Prosecutors said Merchant paid $5,000 to the undercover hitmen as part of an initial payment. He was arrested in July 2024 in Texas as he was about to leave the US.

In court, Merchant argued that he "had no other option" than to go along with the plot because his handler indicated that he knew who Merchant's Iranian relatives were and where they lived.

He said he had anticipated being arrested before anyone was killed and intended to cooperate with the US government, CBS, the BBC's US partner reported. He had hoped that would help him obtain a green card.

The jury deliberated for less than two hours before returning its verdict, US media said.

"An Iranian agent who came to the United States to coordinate an assassination attempt against President Trump has been justly sentenced for his criminal conduct," Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement.

"The Department of Justice will not tolerate foreign terrorist plots against our leaders and will not stop until those responsible are held accountable."

#48. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#45. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from all US freeways

Waymo has temporarily suspended its driverless vehicles from operating on all US freeways, the company has said.

The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless taxis from state freeways following a series of recent traffic incidents.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV) cited an "imminent hazard to public safety" for the ban, noting an incident this month in which several driverless Waymo vehicles entered an active construction zone on US Route 101.

Waymo said it was working with state officials "diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations", adding that no injuries or collisions were reported following the incident on US Route 101 south of San Francisco.

California's DMV said Waymo's driverless taxis presented a risk "due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present".

In another incident earlier this month, footage widely seen on social media appeared to show a Waymo car trying to overtake patrol vehicles that were deliberately slowing the pace of traffic on a San Francisco highway.

The California ban, which came into effect on 11 November, would remain in place until Waymo "demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions".

A Waymo spokesperson said: "In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

Data on Waymo's website suggests that its cars, compared to human drivers, are involved in 95% fewer crashes resulting in serious injury.

Waymo currently operates driverless taxis in 15 US cities, but only offered freeway rides in a handful of those locations, including San Francisco, Los Angeles and Miami.

The company's driverless taxis can continue to operate on regular city streets.

In May, Waymo recalled thousands of its self-driving cars in the US over a software issue that could allow vehicles to drive into flooded roads.

In the same month, multiple Waymo cars were spotted driving around a cul-de-sac in Atlanta, Georgia, in the early hours of the morning.
