#44. European-made pesticides named in 1,605 poisoning reports in Brazil last year

About a third of cases involved chemicals banned for use on EU farms, according to investigation by Unearthed and Public Eye

Pesticides sold by European chemical companies were named in more than 1,600 poisoning reports in Brazil last year, an investigation has found.

About a third of the cases involved chemicals banned from use on farms in the EU because of the risks they pose to people and the environment. But European companies are not banned from manufacturing these chemicals, as long as they export them overseas to countries where they are legally allowed to be used.

Analysis of Brazilian health ministry data by Unearthed and Public Eye in a report published on Tuesday found that of 2,857 poisoning reports that named an identifiable agricultural product, 1,605 named brands sold by European pesticide companies.

Farm workers in Brazil have described their flesh rotting, skin burning, difficulty breathing and vomiting after using the chemicals at work, according to the report. A small number have died accidentally or killed themselves by drinking the pesticides.

Under Brazilian law, reports must be submitted by healthcare professionals to Brazil’s national disease notification system in all suspected pesticide poisoning cases. These reports can be marked “confirmed” poisoning or “exposure” only by the doctor or nurse who saw the patient.

Clinicians sometimes record cases as exposure when they lack the training or support to confirm a diagnosis, but are presented with a patient who says they have been exposed to pesticides or present symptoms of pesticide poisoning. Laboratory confirmation is rare, so recording a diagnosis of “confirmed” poisoning usually depends on clinical judgment.

Experts say clinicians can be reluctant to confirm cases because of a lack of training or support. A brand being named in a poisoning report is not proof that it caused poisoning or death.

“The insufficient training of healthcare professionals to recognise and manage these cases is a major limiting factor,” said Vanda Garibotti, a public health biologist at the State Centre for Health Surveillance in Rio Grande do Sul, southern Brazil.

High staff turnover and a lack of institutional support meant poisoning cases were rarely confirmed through laboratory tests and often relied on clinical judgment, Garibotti added.

In more than 6,700 medical reports involving agricultural pesticides last year, 2,857 named an identifiable product. Of those, 1,420 were classified as confirmed poisonings. A further 1,108 were classified as exposures; for the remainder the classification was unknown or other diagnoses.

Some of the products are made in Britain. Reglone is the most frequently cited UK-made weedkiller. It contains diquat, a chemical that was banned in the UK in 2020 after experts concluded it posed a high risk to people living near fields where it was sprayed. Diquat is also found in other pesticides.

Despite the usage ban, Reglone is still manufactured by the Swiss-headquartered pesticides company Syngenta at its factory in Huddersfield for export to countries with more permissive regulations, including Brazil.

Nelson Castoldi died last year after accidentally ingesting Reglone on his family farm in Rio Grande do Sul. To save money, his family had bought the weedkiller in bulk, decanting a single litre into an empty cola bottle to avoid hauling a heavy 20-litre canister into the fields.

Working under the scorching sun, Castoldi mistook the black chemical for a soft drink and took a sip. Though he spat it out immediately, the chemical caused his kidneys to fail before attacking his lungs. He died two days later.

“We fell apart,” Ivan Castoldi said of his father’s death. “Four months after our father passed away, our mother also passed away. She cried day and night. She became depressive and had a stroke. She was left alone.

“That poison, Reglone, should be banned in Brazil,” he added. “Not because of what happened to us, but because of its toxicity.”

The Brazilian health ministry recorded 75 cases involving diquat last year, compared with 36 over the five years from 2018 to 2022.

The investigation found Reglone was specifically named in 44 reports, including four deaths. Of those cases, 26 were classified as confirmed poisonings and the rest were classified as exposure, unknown, or adverse reaction.

Dormex, a pesticide manufactured in Germany by Alzchem and distributed in Brazil by the German chemical company BASF, is another product workers said injured them or made them ill.

Sidnei Souza de Oliveira, 49, suffered a severe injury after a jug of Dormex spilled on his leg while he was filling a sprayer. The handle broke as he was transferring the chemical into the tank. At first, his leg turned red, and he continued spraying.

He carried on working for four days before going to hospital. “After 15 days, my leg was black. Chunks of flesh were falling off,” Oliveira said. A doctor said the injury was serious and had to remove dead tissue to stop it spreading towards the bone.

Oliveira said the protective trousers supplied by his employer were thin and allowed the chemical to seep through. He said he had been afraid of losing his job if he stayed away from work for too long.

Dormex contains cyanamide, which was banned from use in the EU in 2008 because of concerns about its effects on human health, particularly among farm workers spraying the chemical. Analysis shows the Brazilian health ministry database recorded 17 confirmed poisonings involving Dormex last year.

Finale, a BASF product identified in the investigation, contains glufosinate, which was banned in the EU in 2018. Roundup – a glyphosate-based weedkiller manufactured by the German chemical company Bayer – was frequently cited in Brazilian poisoning reports, including 12 deaths.

Across all the poisoning reports identified by the investigation as involving products sold by European companies, there were 32 reported deaths. Of these cases, 31 were classified as confirmed poisonings, and 26 were recorded as suicides.

Syngenta products were cited 425 times in the poisoning reports, and BASF 179 times. The company figures are not mutually exclusive: some poisoning reports named products sold by more than one manufacturer.

Nina Schrank, a senior campaigner at Greenpeace UK, said the findings were a “deadly double standard”.

“If a pesticide is too dangerous to use in Britain, it should be too dangerous to export. Ministers must stop allowing Britain to be part of a production line for toxic chemicals that are putting lives at risk overseas.”

The EU’s pesticide approval system requires chemicals to meet strict standards for human and animal health and the environment. Diquat has been banned on EU and UK farms since 2019 and 2020 respectively, after experts concluded it posed a high risk to workers, people living nearby and birds. Cyanamide was withdrawn after an assessment found exposure among agricultural operators could exceed safe levels. Glufosinate, meanwhile, is classified in the EU as toxic to reproduction, meaning it can harm fertility or the development of unborn children.

Suicide prevention is also a factor. A 2020 study found national bans on highly hazardous pesticides could be a cost-effective intervention for reducing the number of people who kill themselves in countries with a high burden of suicides attributable to pesticides.

A Syngenta spokesperson said almost half the Brazilian cases attributed to the company were not classified as confirmed poisonings, adding: “Public Eye and Unearthed are aware of this but have explicitly chosen to disregard the dataset’s final classification.” The company accused the investigation of disregarding the dataset’s final classifications and identified “numerous other inconsistencies” that it said raised “serious concerns” that the evidence was “being selectively interpreted to support a predetermined conclusion”.

“There are real people behind these statistics. Every genuine case of poisoning matters, and every family affected deserves our concern,” the spokesperson added.

Syngenta said Brazil had its own “robust regulatory system” and that products banned in Europe could still be independently assessed and legally registered in the country, citing differences in crops, growing conditions and pest pressures. It said it trained 95,000 people in Brazil in the safe use of its products in 2025, including through free online courses.

Bayer said its products were safe when used according to label instructions, citing what it described as rigorous testing and assessments by global health authorities. The company said it took pesticide poisoning seriously but questioned the reliability of the data, arguing that products and circumstances of exposure had not been independently verified, some chemicals may have been incorrectly identified and symptoms such as headaches and skin irritation could have other causes.

It said “Roundup” was “widely used as a generic term” that could refer to weedkillers made by other manufacturers or counterfeit products.

The company said it provided training on the safe handling and use of pesticides, including protective equipment, and that its programmes reached more than 4 million people globally in 2025. It also argued that a pesticide being banned in the EU did not necessarily mean it was unsafe, saying other countries had robust regulatory systems that took account of local agricultural conditions.

It added: “While the data presented helps to provide information on broad trends of exposure, the data has limitations and warrants caution when drawing conclusions, particularly when looking at individual cases.”

BASF said it was deeply concerned by reports of poisoning that might be linked to its products, adding that it took all such incidents seriously.

A spokesperson said publicly available information suggested some incidents might have involved the intentional ingestion of crop protection products, describing such acts as “deliberate misuse” of products intended exclusively for agricultural use.

BASF said it had global and local procedures to assess and investigate incidents potentially involving its products, and worked with authorities and experts where appropriate to understand cases and identify ways to prevent similar incidents. It said it invested in education and training to promote the safe handling and use of its products, with initiatives in Brazil reaching more than 14,475 participants in 2025.

The company said it was confident in the safety of its products when used in accordance with label instructions and applicable regulatory standards.

It added that Brazilian law required crop protection products to be handled and applied only by authorised and properly trained individuals, and said it took its responsibility for ensuring their safe use seriously.

#41. Costa Rican journalist shot dead in first such killing in 25 years

Camilo Rodriguez’s death comes as the country grapples with a surge in drug violence

The Costa Rican journalist Camilo Rodríguez, who gained prominence for publishing allegations against ⁠suspected drug traffickers and politicians, was shot dead on Monday, authorities said.

The first known killing of a journalist ⁠in Costa Rica in ⁠25 years, ​Rodriguez’s death comes as the country grapples with a surge in drug violence driven by international trafficking groups, despite long ⁠being regarded as one of Central America’s most peaceful and stable democracies.

Rodriguez, 51, was attacked at about 3.20pm local ⁠time amid heavy traffic on a main highway on the outskirts of the capital ​San José, the Red Cross ‌reported.

President Laura Fernández condemned ‌the attack in a video message.

“I am filled with indignation and outrage over ‌the cowardly murder of communicator Camilo Rodríguez. Regardless of the content of his communications, what happened today in our country is a stain on freedom of expression,” Fernández said.

The attorney general, Carlo Díaz, told the local news outlet Extra Noticias that investigators found multiple bullet impacts and a crime scene resembling a contract ‌killing, probably linked to the allegations Rodríguez had published. He gave no further evidence and did not name suspects.

“We know he ​was a communicator who publicly identified people for alleged links to drug trafficking,” Díaz said, adding that Rodríguez had previously reported threats but had declined protection.

“Silencing someone who reports on drug trafficking or corruption, in this case a communicator, is a ⁠signal we cannot tolerate in a democratic society.“

Díaz pledged efforts to ​hold those responsible ​for the murder accountable.

The last such ​attack on a media worker happened in July 2001 when the radio ​producer Parmenio ‌Medina was gunned ​down on a ​Santo Domingo highway, in roughly the same area as Rodríguez’s attack took place.

Costa Rica’s homicide rate doubled between 2013 and 2023 and has only eased slightly since then.

#39. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#35. Eight-year-old Haitian boy returns home after release from ICE custody

Child reunited with family after he was detained by immigration agents in San Diego and transferred to Texas

An eight-year-old Haitian boy who was put into restraints and detained by immigration agents in San Diego on 4 November and transferred to a facility in Texas has been reunited with his family in California.

Representative Juan Vargas shared a photograph of the boy and his family in a social media post on Monda, writing: “11 days ago, an 8-year-old in San Diego was taken into federal custody during an immigration check-in. He was separated from his loved ones and held in a facility in Texas. Today, he’s finally home.”

The Haitian Bridge Alliance, an immigration advocacy group, confirmed that the third-grader had been released from Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) custody.

“An eight-year-old child belongs in a classroom, on a playground, and surrounded by love – not in immigration detention. We welcome his release, but his freedom should never have required a public outcry,” Guerline Jozef, executive director of the Haitian Bridge Alliance, said in a statement.

The third-grader at Fay elementary school was put in restraints and taken into custody by federal immigration officers at a check-in earlier in the month, according to video obtained by NBC News and an oversight letter Democratic lawmakers sent to the Departments of Homeland Security and Health and Human Services.

A spokesperson for the homeland security department said ICE took the child into custody “out of an abundance of caution and to ensure the child’s safety”.

The uncle, the spokesperson said in a statement to the Guardian, was unable to prove legal guardianship – and that officials were obliged by federal anti-trafficking laws to take the boy into care as an “unaccompanied alien child”.

The Haitian Bridge Alliance said the boy was in the care of his uncle and cousins after his father was deported last year.

“I’m so grateful that he’s home, but this should have never happened in the first place,” Vargas said in a second social media post, adding that he would be asking the Trump administration for answers. While he was detained, the child was “held in a facility in Texas, far away from his home in San Diego”, Vargas said.

Celebrating the boy’s return, Jozef of the Haitian Bridge Alliance told NBC News: “The aunt was literally crying, and she said: ‘I have not eaten since the Wednesday they took my baby.’ They haven’t been able to sleep because of that.” She added: “The cousins were there when they took him, and wondering: will they ever see him again? But today [Monday] they are able to see him again. They are able to hug him.”

#33. British hacker who stole $11m through text message scheme jailed for six years in US

Spanish police arrested Buchanan in Madrid

A British hacker has been sentenced to six years in a federal prison in the US for conspiring to hack into at least a dozen companies via text message phishing, and stealing more than $11.6m (Â£8.8m) in cryptocurrency from individual victims.

Tyler Buchanan, 24, from Dundee, started hacking as a teenager and was part of the collective of young hackers known as Scattered Spider, which has been blamed for a string of high profile cyber-crimes.

US prosecutors said the thefts had "severe, life-altering consequences" for victims and showed the growing threat posed by online crime groups.

The sentence brings to an end a years-long international investigation involving authorities in the UK, US, Spain and Canada.

Buchanan admitted taking part in a hacking campaign that began in 2021, when he was 19.

Working from his parents' home, he and other English-speaking hackers in the US and UK targeted employees at major technology and telecommunications companies.

It made them $8m in less than two years, according to US prosecutors.

Buchanan and his hacker gang sent out hundreds of SMS messages to the mobile phones of people who worked for large tech or telecoms companies.

The SMS phishing messages contained links to spoof websites designed to look like legitimate sites of victim companies.

The group used messages like "Hi, your account is due to expire - you need to log in to your work account here immediately or you will lose access to work apps".

The websites then lured the recipient into providing confidential information, including personal identifying information (PII), and account usernames and passwords.

The boys and young men did it on an industrial scale with Buchanan setting up a Telegram bot to collect all the victims' credentials as the duped workers unwittingly handed over their login details.

For some victim companies, subsequently stolen information included confidential work information, intellectual property, plus account access credentials, names, email addresses, and telephone numbers.

The group then used stolen information and SIM-swapping techniques to target cryptocurrency investors in the United States.

In the largest single theft, prosecutors said one victim lost $1.67m in December 2022.

Overall, authorities calculated that Buchanan and his co-conspirators stole more than $11.6m from victims.

Buchanan was also a victim of cyber-criminals.

In early 2023 his online identity and home address were leaked by rival hackers seeking access to his cryptocurrency.

Cyber security researchers and journalists reported that he was assaulted at his home and threatened with a blowtorch.

"TylerB and his group were highly sophisticated and organised but the violence he and others experienced was a sign of the troubling direction of these online communities of young hackers" says cyber security researcher Allison Nixon from Unit221B.

Buchanan then reportedly fled to Canada to escape further threats before returning home.

In April 2023, officers from Police Scotland searched Buchanan's bedroom after receiving a request from the FBI.

Investigators recovered a device containing the names and addresses of hacking victims, along with cryptocurrency seed phrases and account credentials belonging to at least one victim.

The following month, Buchanan left the UK again.

He was arrested on 31 May 2024 at Palma de Mallorca Airport in Spain while attempting to board a private flight to Naples.

After being extradited to the United States, he remained in custody before being sentenced this week.

In his November 2023 charge, Buchanan was named along with four American men and boys in their twenties and teens for alleged activity linked to the notorious cyber-criminal collective Scattered Spider.

They have all pleaded guilty too and are now serving time in jail.

Two other members of the Scattered Spider group were jailed in the UK in July for various cyber attacks including a disruptive one against Transport for London that caused Â£39m of damage.

#29. Grand Theft Auto 6 is a masterpiece, say critics

Review scores for GTA 6 are a fairytale ending after years of development

After 13 years of waiting, record-breaking trailers and lengthy delays, critics have given their answer to one of the year's biggest questions.

Does Grand Theft Auto 6, surely the most anticipated game of all time, live up to the hype?

According to the first reviews, it's a resounding yes.

Metacritic, which aggregates reviews from critics, averages it at 99 out of 100, external - tying with Nintendo's The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, which has held the record since 1998.

Reviewers overwhelmingly agreed the open-world action game was an ambitious, ground-breaking title that was well worth the wait.

Not everyone is enjoying the glowing praise - with some posters to online forums objecting to what one called "the ultra fan-boy hype."

But there is no question the reviewers have been effusive with their praise, with gaming website IGN, external branding GTA 6 "unprecedented", "unbelievable" and "unmissable".

Meanwhile in its five star review, Eurogamer said makers Rockstar had "pushed back the horizon of video games once again" with its graphics, storytelling and world building.

"The awe is all part of the point, of course, and GTA 6 is nothing if not truly awesome," reviewer Chris Tapsell wrote, external, but he added "there are also nits to pick".

For The New York Times, external one of those nits was whether the realism of the game might dull the fun chaos of its predecessors.

Reviewer Yussef Cole said GTA 6 had "matured beyond its memorable car crashes and stickups" with consequences to every action.

But "some of my favourite moments have been careening through streets with squad cars in hot pursuit," he wrote.

Critics say the relationship between Jason and Lucia is central to the game

The graphics of the game were hotly anticipated and so far have not disappointed critics, with The Guardian, external describing Leonida, the setting inspired by Miami, as "so lifelike that at times it beggars belief".

And the relationship between the lead characters, Jason and Lucia, has also stunned critics.

"They feel like real people rather than puppets dancing to your whim," The Guardian's Keza MacDonald wrote, adding: "I found it impossible not to care for them".

Writing for Forbes, external, Paul Tassi said: "I can't say I've ever seen a better video game relationship than the one they share".

The Washington Post, external said GTA 6 "raises the bar at every level", while Bloomberg, external's Jason Schreier called it a "masterpiece" in his five star review.

"It's hard not to be astonished by what Rockstar has done here," he wrote. "There's nothing else like it.

"While playing this game, I often found myself wondering how it even exists."

Rockstar hasn't revealed the cost of the game, but some estimates put the figure north of $1bn (Â£760m).

GTA 6 is the most hotly anticipated videogame ever

The Game Awards - the most watched ceremony of its kind in the industry - gave players a teaser of the critical reception when its 2026 nominations were revealed on Tuesday.

And despite having not released at the time, GTA 6 was the most-nominated title.

It will be up for eight prizes, including game of the year and best music.

The nomination of Manni L Perez in the best performance category confirmed wide speculation that she is the actress behind main character Lucia in GTA 6 - a fact Rockstar hadn't previously officially confirmed.

The jury for the event is largely made up of gaming journalists around the world, who would have received advanced copies for review.

Some, though, have questioned how a game that has just been released has garnered such positive reviews and award nominations.

There is a view in some online forums that the hype around the game has got out of control as, with one poster commenting: "I think everyone's tired of hearing about this game - I don't even want to play it anymore."

It appears so far, however, that is likely to be very much a minority view.

#27. Isolated Brazilian tribe emerges amid alarm over threats to group’s way of life

Indigenous Awá people of Amazon make contact with villagers and accept food to take back to territory

About 20 Indigenous people emerged from isolation in Brazil’s Amazon rainforest, several organizations reported on Tuesday, raising the alarm about increased invasions into the tribe’s territory.

According to the NGO Survival International, members of the Awá people, who live in voluntary isolation, arrived on Monday in a village and made contact with a government team in a “historic encounter”.

The village they entered was within the Arariboia Indigenous territory in the north-eastern state of Maranhão, home to the Guajajara ethnic group.

Local community leaders said the villagers offered food to the group.

“They asked for yams and took them back into their forest,” said Survival International.

The NGO explained that the Awá typically subsist on hunting and gathering. However, they have faced increased incursions from illegal loggers, hunters and cattle ranchers.

A video of the encounter, filmed by Guajajara villagers and released by Survival International, shows a group of men, women and children at the edge of the forest.

Partially concealed by vegetation, they appear naked and are wearing necklaces. The men carry bows and long arrows.

“The physical condition of some members, particularly the children and the elderly, caused concern among the villagers, who noted clear signs of severe emaciation,” warned Coapima, an organization representing Indigenous groups in Maranhão.

Coapima highlighted the risks that unplanned contact poses to such groups, noting that they are particularly “exposed to diseases” against which they have little to no immunity.

Funai, the public agency responsible for Indigenous affairs, said in a statement sent to AFP that its technical teams were closely monitoring the situation and analyzing available information regarding the group’s health.

Survival International’s director, Caroline Pearce, said the group’s choice to emerge from isolation was a “troubling indication of how the continued invasion of uncontacted peoples’ forests puts otherwise self-sufficient Indigenous people in real danger”.

She said the Awá were being driven into “an ever-smaller island of the Amazon rainforest, surrounded by destruction”.

According to the NGO, about 196 “uncontacted” peoples and groups, who choose to live in isolation, have been identified across 10 countries.

More than 90% of such groups live in the Amazon rainforest, primarily in Brazil, with others in Indonesia and India.

The Forest Guardians – a group of Guajajara people who have battled illegal invasions for years – warned in comments in the Survival International statement that “climate change, fires, deforestation and land encroachment” were affecting the Awá’s way of life.

According to Survival International, many of the guardians have lost their lives trying to protect the territory they share with the Awá.

#25. Bancroft sandpaper claims heap pressure on Cummins

Cameron Bancroft and Steve Smith were banned for their part in the sandpaper plot

Cameron Bancroft has claimed the whole Australia team knew about the 2018 sandpaper plot in explosive revelations that will heap pressure on current captain Pat Cummins.

In a detailed testimony, published by the West Australian,, external batter Bancroft - the player caught with the sandpaper during the Test match against South Africa - has given arguably the most complete account of the events in Cape Town eight years ago.

"In 2018 not the entire truth was told," said Bancroft. "I have never been completely honest about what happened."

For the past two weeks, Australian cricket has been embroiled in a debate over the ball-tampering saga following the publication of Usman Khawaja's autobiography.

Khawaja wrote that the whole Australia team knew of the plot, claims supported by David Warner and Chadd Sayers. Cummins described Khawaja's account as "wrong" and "nonsense".

On the day Cricket Australia announced a forum into the sandpaper episode, Bancroft published his account, explaining he was motivated by the news conference given by Cummins.

"The final straw for me to write this account was Australian cricket captain Pat Cummins' press conference," said Bancroft.

"It made me feel angry, betrayed and alone.

"What I saw, experienced and lived with - not only him, but everyone else on that tour in South Africa - is not the public account he gave in his press conference and in the years previously when he has spoken on this time."

Opener Bancroft was given a nine-month ban for his part in the plot.

Steve Smith was stripped of the Australia captaincy and given a year-long ban, the same suspension given to vice-captain Warner. No other players or coaches were punished.

In his more than 4,000-word piece, Bancroft has claimed:

Sandpaper was first used during the second Test against South Africa â the match before Bancroft was caught

Bancroft had conversations with the Australia seamers â Cummins, Mitchell Starc, Josh Hazlewood and Mitchell Marsh about whether the ball was "swinging enough"

Before the third day of the second Test, the entire playing group and staff had "celebrations" about the effect of the sandpaper

When Bancroft was caught during the third Test, then Australia coach Darren Lehmann told 12th man Peter Handscomb to "get the sandpaper off Bangers now"

Bancroft found the publication of a joint-statement by the Australia bowlers in 2021 "deeply painful".

Khawaja's autobiography and the subsequent claims made by Warner and seamer Sayers have reopened old wounds related to the sandpaper saga.

Still, those connected have remained true to the accounts they have presented during the intervening eight years.

The most significant denial came from Cummins, who rebuffed Khawaja's account in an extraordinary 68-minute address to the media in Sydney. Lehmann and Starc have also reiterated their denials.

Now, it is the extensive detail provided by Bancroft that could shed a whole new light on the controversy and lead to further questions for Cummins, Lehmann and Cricket Australia.

Cummins is due to lead Australia in the first Test against New Zealand on 9 December.

A statement released on behalf of Cummins said: "Whilst we feel for Cam and everything he's been through there's nothing in the statement that hasn't been answered by Pat in his press conference."

A Cricket Australia statement said: "We respect that Cameron Bancroft has told his story, and we will take time to consider his comments.

"We expect that there will be many dimensions to the events of 2018, which is why we have established the forum for individuals to give their version of events.

"We are finalising the terms of reference and will commence the process as soon as possible."

In his account, Bancroft said: "I have spent eight years speaking about accountability, honesty and the lessons I learned from one of Australian cricket's darkest moments.

"However, in 2018 not the entire truth was told. I have never been completely honest about what happened.

"It was not just the three of us â Steve Smith, David Warner and I â who knew about me using sandpaper on the ball in South Africa. It was the whole team.

"When I was questioned during Cricket Australia's investigation, I did not give a complete and truthful account.

"I withheld important details, including when the use of sandpaper began, how long it went on for, and the involvement and awareness of others.

"I did this to protect Cricket Australia's investigation and not implicate others around me."

'No way in a million years' the whole team didn't know - Vaughan on sandpaper scandal

Bancroft said his actions were "discussed" and "acknowledged" by the entire playing group at the end of the second day and before the beginning of the third day. He said he was encouraged by Saker, who has since worked as a consultant with England.

The only person within the camp to express unease at the ball-tampering, according to Bancroft, was media manager Kate Hutchison.

"I received wraps and encouragement from members of the playing and coaching staff, especially David Saker," said Bancroft.

"Our media manager, Kate Hutchison, expressed her objection to what was happening.

"I recall her making it clear that she believed it was wrong, that she wanted nothing to do with it, and walking away from the discussion.

"I will never forget that. Somebody had spoken up.

"Somebody had demonstrated that being part of the team did not mean having to accept everything the team was doing. And I ignored it."

Bancroft identifies Saker as the coach Khawaja claimed encouraged him to work harder on the ball during the third Test in Cape Town. The 33-year-old said he took "feedback" from all the bowlers and wicketkeeper Tim Paine, who took over the captaincy when Smith was sacked.

In Cape Town, Bancroft was confronted by the umpires when TV cameras showed him concealing the sandpaper in his trousers. He claimed Lehmann sent a message to him via Handscomb.

"Peter Handscomb ran out at the drinks break after I'd been caught and shoved the sandpaper down my pants," wrote Bancroft.

"He said to me: 'I think it's time I take that off you now.' And Pete was doing his job after Boof [Lehmanm] shouted to him on the walkie talkie "get the sandpaper off Bangers [Bancroft] now".

"If he didn't know he wouldn't have said that. And if it wasn't clear enough before, then it was obvious now."

In 2021, Bancroft was playing county cricket with Durham and gave an interview to The Guardian in which he said the bowlers' involvement in the plot was "self-explantory".

In response, Cummins, Starc, Hazlewood and spinner Nathan Lyon released a joint-statement denying any knowledge that ball-tampering had taken place.

"Reading that statement was deeply painful," said Bancroft.

"I had memories of conversations on the field in Port Elizabeth and Newlands, feedback about the ball and shared celebrations that made the statement difficult for me to digest."

'Cricket Australia didn't get it right' - Gillespie

For almost two weeks, Australian cricket has been at war with itself over the revelations in Usman Khawaja's autobiography.

But there has been an absence of a smoking gun. Without tangible, new, hard evidence, all that was left was claim and counter-claim.

Naturally, the spotlight fell on Pat Cummins because of his role as a bowler in Cape Town and his elevation to Australia captain in the years since.

His performance last week in front of the media, on live television, was extraordinary. For 68 minutes he took all questions and appeared plausible in his answers.

In being so robust in his denial of Khawaja's account - Cummins called it "nonsense" - the skipper also left himself open to the consequences if it was later revealed he was not telling the truth.

Crucially, it was Cummins' press conference that compelled Cameron Bancroft to speak out.

It must be stressed that Bancroft's account is not the open-and-shut evidence that convicts Cummins or anyone else in the dressing room at Newlands in 2018.

But it is certainly the most complete account we have heard from anyone involved, walking step-by-step through the genesis of the plot, the execution and the fallout. Bancroft has named names: Cummins, the other bowlers, Tim Paine, coach Darren Lehmann and assistant coach David Saker. It is, potentially, the decisive intervention that shifts this whole saga in a new direction.

Whether by accident or design, it came on the day Cricket Australia (CA) announced its forum for evidence into the sandpaper controversy. Before Bancroft's account CA's action felt an unsatisfactory halfway house - not an investigation, with no guarantee outcomes will be published.

What now? Will CA go further? How will those implicated by Bancroft respond? How can Cummins lead Australia in the first Test against New Zealand? Could Steve Smith break a silence he has held since the publication of Khawaja's book?

Throughout the past fortnight, there has been a sense that Australian cricket hoped the sandpaper debate would eventually go away. Cummins even said he never wanted to speak about it again.

Instead of fading, the thirst for the truth grows, now fuelled by Bancroft's extraordinary story.

#23. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting

#19. Democrats win Alaska Senate race two weeks after midterms counting began

Mary Peltola defeated the state's Republican incumbent after a controversial race

Democrat Mary Peltola has won Alaska's hard-fought Senate race, completing a sweeping victory for the party in the US upper house in the midterm elections.

The win gives the Democrats a 56th Senate seat following a tumultuous and controversial election in Alaska, which featured alleged spoiler recruitment efforts and unprecedented court battles.

It took two full weeks of counting to call a winner in the 3 November poll due to the logistical challenge of collecting ballots from across the state's remote expanse.

It is the ninth Senate seat the Democrats have taken from Republicans in the midterms, giving the party control of the US Congress for the last two years of Donald Trump's presidency.

The Democrats also won back the House of Representatives, the lower chamber.

Peltola defeated the incumbent Republican senator, Dan S Sullivan, who is yet to comment.

In her victory statement, Peltola said the "status quo in DC is not working for Alaska".

She recalled speaking to "elders in tears about empty fish racks" and to parents in rural Alaska choosing between heating their homes and putting food on the table.

Peltola is the first Alaska Native elected to the US Senate, and is the first Democrat to win a US Senate seat in Alaska since 2008.

Donald Trump won the state by 13 points in the 2024 presidential election, but defeat for the Republicans in this year's US Senate race in the state caps off a string of losses in the midterms for the president's party.

Many voters across the United States cited cost-of-living issues as a factor that affected how they cast their ballots.

Trump says Alaska voters chose the wrong Dan Sullivan. What does the data show?

Could Alaska's fishing industry tilt Washington's balance of power?

Peltola's campaign had come under fire after the New York Times, external reported that her team worked with people who tried to recruit so-called spoiler candidates in an alleged attempt to confuse voters.

The alleged scheme led to a candidate named Daniel J Sullivan - a complete political unknown residing in a small coastal town who shared a name with the state's current US Senator and incumbent, Dan S Sullivan - contesting the race.

After legal challenges, state courts ruled that Daniel J Sullivan could remain on the ballot, and election officials distinguished them on election materials.

Peltola denied having knowledge of the alleged plot but her campaign swiftly came under fire, and both the US justice department and Alaska's Republican governor said they would launch investigations.

The Trump administration also filed a lawsuit attempting to stop the certification of the election results if the incumbent Republican Senator Sullivan lost.

A federal judge, though, dismissed the case, following legal challenges from Alaska election officials as well as the Peltola campaign and voting groups.

Due to Alaska's ranked-choice voting system, voters confused about which Dan Sullivan was the incumbent could have simply chosen both men, listing them as their first and second choices.

If they did so, after the first round of voting was complete, tabulation would have re-allocated any votes from Daniel J Sullivan - who stood little to no chance of winning - to Dan S Sullivan.

Experts told the BBC that this process made it difficult for any potential spoiler candidate to have any impact on the results.

An analysis by BBC Verify found that even if every vote for Daniel J Sullivan had gone to the incumbent, Dan S Sullivan, Peltola would still have won.

The Democratic win in Alaska cements the party's majority in the US Senate, as the party now has 56 seats to the Republicans' 44.

The most senior Democrat in the Senate, Chuck Schumer, said Peltola had run "a pitch-perfect campaign devoted to the issues Alaskans care most about". Hailing the Democrats' final tally in the Senate, he said: "56 seats was an unimaginable result even just a few months ago."

The Alaska seat was initially one of several toss-up races that appeared as a must-win for Democrats if they wanted to gain control of the US Senate. But by the time Peltola was called the winner, they had seats to spare.

With 99% of ballots counted, Peltola had slightly more than 50% of the votes, while Sullivan trailed on less than 46%.

Sullivan, a former Alaska attorney general and an ally of Trump, campaigned on his record of delivering energy and military investment for the state.

Peltola promised Alaskans a more independent voice in Washington.

"As a lifelong Alaskan who has dedicated my life to serving our state, there is no greater honor than having Alaskans' support in our fight to take on corporate greed, lower costs, and put Alaska first," she said in her victory statement.

"I will stand up to anyone to take on the rigged system in DC and deliver results for Alaska's working families."

#18. Relocation of Cape Town baboons to sanctuary ignites heated protests

Demonstrators tried to block vehicle carrying the baboons, whose capture authorities say is needed to protect both primate and human

The relocation of a troop of baboons in Cape Town to a wildlife sanctuary has triggered furious protests by local people opposed to their capture, which officials argue is necessary to stop human-wildlife conflict in the city.

The 44-member Waterfall troop (named after the area they mainly inhabit) was captured in the southern seaside suburb of Simon’s Town on Friday and Saturday. A second, smaller Seaforth troop is also due to be moved.

The growth in both baboon and human populations in Cape Town in recent years has led to increased conflict and more baboons dying as a result. Meanwhile, a heated debate has raged between people who believe that baboons and humans can coexist and those who argue that baboons in urban areas are in danger and in some cases need to be relocated. Some animal rights activists have compared authorities’ baboon management to apartheid.

During Friday’s relocation, outraged protesters tried to stop vehicles taking the baboons away. One person sprayed a fire extinguisher, according to the Daily Maverick, a local news outlet.

After the confrontation, the local Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals branch urged those opposed to the baboons’ capture not to try to stop the vehicles. “Capture and transport are already stressful for the baboons,” it said in a statement. “Delaying their arrival prolongs their time in crates and risks adding to that stress – this is not fair on the baboons.”

It said it remained opposed to the baboon’s relocation, adding: “Our presence did not imply support … our inspectors are present because the animals need someone looking out for them.”

On Sunday, dozens of people sat outside the wildlife centre in a silent protest, some tying flowers to the fence. Baboons from a different troop also gathered at the boundary, calling to the newly captive animals, the Daily Maverick reported.

“We stand together for the Seaforth and Waterfall troops, for their freedom, their dignity and their right to live as wild animals,” Mandy Neal, the administrator of the Save Kogelfontein Baboons Facebook group, posted on Monday, calling for weekly Sunday silent protests. “ Our message is simple: CAPTIVITY IS NOT CONSERVATION.”

The City of Cape Town said in a statement on Sunday that the relocation had been successful, with the baboons being monitored 24/7 after being checked by vets and the females receiving contraceptive implants.

It said: “The Waterfall troop has been relocated due to the severity and persistence of human-baboon conflict in the Simon’s Town urban environment, the limited available natural low-lying habitat, the increasing risk to the baboons’ welfare, and public safety concerns … [and] as a very last resort after other interventions were evaluated and deemed ineffective, and as an alternative to culling.”

Prof Justin O’Riain, a behavioural ecologist at the University of Cape Town, said the baboon management plan had undergone two years of public consultations. “It has thus been disappointing watching members of the public and select NGOs, many which did not engage in the stakeholder process, now compromising the safety and welfare of the baboons and people through violent actions directed at staff and baboons in transit.”

#15. US basketball player arrested with 75lb of marijuana at Nigerian airport, say authorities

Drug smuggling carries life sentence in Nigeria

An American basketball player has been arrested in Nigeria for allegedly attempting to smuggle 33.95kg (75lb) of marijuana into the country.

Nigeria’s National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLEA) said a 28-year-old player named as Greene Da’monte Khiree from the state of Georgia was arrested at Lagos’s main airport last Wednesday after he arrived on a flight from Thailand via the United Arab Emirates.

The NDLEA said the player told them he had come to Nigeria to “build networks” for his basketball career. The agency said evidence on his phone suggested he had “knowledge of and involvement in the drug trafficking activity”. The NDLEA said it detained the player when he attempted to leave the airport with his hand luggage after abandoning two checked-in bags. The agency said those two bags were found to contain 32 parcels of marijuana, and a tracking device.

Nigeria has strict drug laws, and trafficking carries a sentence of life in prison.

A basketball player by the name of Damonte Greene is listed on the roster of the Pattani Raptors in the Thai Premier Men’s League. Greene, a Georgia native, played college basketball for Reinhardt University and South Georgia Technical College. He played semi-professional basketball in the US before representing a number of teams in Thailand.

WNBA star Brittney Griner and former Utah State player Jarred Shaw were imprisoned in Russia and Indonesia respectively for marijuana offenses. Griner was released in December 2022, while Shaw remains in prison.

#14. Ghana's new $60m presidential jet sparks public outrage

The French-made Falcon 6X replaces the ageing presidential jet, officials say

Ghana's government has defended its purchase of a new presidential jet amid criticism over its cost, as many citizens continue to struggle with the high cost of living.

The $60m (Â£46m) French-made Falcon 6X has sparked a political row, with opposition politicians accusing President John Mahama's administration of misplaced priorities.

But the government says the purchase is justified, arguing that Ghana's improving economy makes the aircraft affordable and is needed to replace an ageing presidential jet.

The controversy comes as Mahama's administration seeks to demonstrate an economic recovery after years of financial difficulties that forced Ghana to seek IMF assistance.

Can Ghana's new president meet the voters' high expectations?

Manufactured by French aerospace company Dassault Aviation, the aircraft was delivered to the Ghana Air Force on Monday at a reported cost of $61m.

Parliament approved its purchase last December as part of a broader programme to modernise Ghana's military aviation fleet, which also includes four helicopters from Airbus.

The jet was welcomed with a water salute on Monday, but its arrival triggered criticism online and among opposition politicians over the cost and timing of the purchase.

While in the opposition, Mahama himself opposed buying a new presidential jet, prompting questions about why his administration has now gone head with the purchase.

Opposition MP Samuel Abu Jinapor questioned the government's priorities, saying the expenditure came at a time when many Ghanaians continue to struggle with the cost of living and youth unemployment remained alarmingly high.

"What was unacceptable in opposition cannot suddenly become acceptable in government merely because the occupants of power have changed," Jinapor added.

He accused the governing National Democratic Congress (NDC) of hypocrisy, recalling how its senior figures had strongly opposed proposals for a new presidential aircraft when they were in opposition.

Jinapor also called for greater transparency over the procurement process, how the purchase was financed and the government's justification for the expenditure.

Defence Minister Kenneth Adjei welcomed and inspected the new jet on Monday

The purchase has also prompted sharp criticism on social media, with some users arguing the money could have been spent on more pressing public needs.

"This money can employ over 100,000 nurses and 200,000 teachers," an X user posted.

But the government has rejected the criticism, saying Ghana's economic circumstances have changed considerably since the NDC opposed a similar purchase.

Peter Lanchene Toobu, chairman of parliament's defence committee, said the party had never opposed acquiring a presidential jet in principle.

Its objection, he said, had been on the timing when Ghana was experiencing severe economic difficulties.

"We agreed in principle that we needed to purchase a new presidential jet," he said.

He argued, however, that Ghana could not justify the expenditure at a time when it was seeking a $3bn bailout from the IMF.

"The strength and macroeconomic stability of this country have enhanced our status in the sense that imports have become cheaper, and this is the right time," he added.

Toobu said the government had purchased the aircraft without borrowing, citing a stronger Ghanaian currency and improved economic conditions.

Government spokesperson Kwakye Ofosu also dismissed suggestions that the purchase was a misplaced priority.

He pointed to falling prices of construction materials and lower borrowing costs as evidence of the country's improved economic situation.

"When I was campaigning in 2024, and I was donating cement, it cost 115 cedis (Â£7.5) a bag. Today, for 80 cedis, you will get a bag of cement," he told JoyNews.

The controversy has also extended to the aircraft's interior, following claims that it has a dedicated bedroom and bathroom.

But Ofosu dismissed claims that the jet had such luxury facilities, saying the president has no intention of "bathing in the air".

"There certainly is no bathroom. President Mahama has no intention of bathing in the air," Ofosu said.

He explained that the jet has reclining seats that can be converted into sleeping arrangements but denied that it has a dedicated bedroom.

The government says the new jet will replace the 16-year-old Falcon 900EX, which has suffered recurring maintenance problems, including corrosion and leakages in its fuel tank.

The older jet was reportedly grounded in France for several months in 2025 while undergoing repairs.

Officials say the Falcon 6X has a longer flying range, reducing the need for refuelling stops on international trips and improving the reliability of presidential travel.

Professor Godfred Alufar Bokpin, an economist at the University of Ghana said that while the president's safety was paramount, the timing of the purchase was difficult to justify amid severe austerity measures, high unemployment and widespread infrastructure challenges.

"The timing makes it a bit difficult to understand," he told the BBC, adding that the government should provide a cost-benefit analysis explaining why buying the jet was preferable to leasing one and how the decision serves Ghana's interests.

Additional reporting by Thomas Naadi in Accra

The far-right National Rally leader has a significant lead in the latest polls.

#10. Five EU states announce plans for immigration ‘return hubs’ in Rwanda

Germany, Austria, Netherlands, Denmark and Greece likely to face challenges to policy criticised as ‘grave moral failing’

Five EU member states have announced plans to set up an offshore centre in Rwanda for people with no legal right to stay in Europe, in a pilot scheme that is expected to face legal challenges and has been denounced as “a grave moral failing”.

The five countries – Germany, Austria, the Netherlands, Denmark and Greece – are the first to seek to use the EU’s newly agreed returns law that allows people who have exhausted all avenues of appeal against their removal from Europe to be sent to a third country.

In a statement released on Monday the five EU countries said “concrete negotiations with partner country Rwanda will begin for implementation”, saying the scheme would make “an important contribution to further curbing illegal migration, dismantling the business model of smugglers and relieving the burden on our population”.

Officials hope to send 100 people to Rwanda from early next year to test the model. The scheme will apply to adults whose claims to asylum have been rejected or others with no legal right to live in the EU.

In theory, according to diplomatic sources, migrants would stay in a reception centre in Rwanda for a couple of months, before choosing to integrate into life in the Great Lakes country or return to their home nation.

Unlike the failed scheme pursued by the British Conservative government in Rwanda, or Italy’s agreement with Albania, it will not apply to people pursuing asylum claims.

The reception centre, which would be an open facility, was not intended to house people long term but to accommodate regular flows of people, the countries said. Diplomatic sources said the EU countries were not using British-funded facilities built in Kigali.

The governments involved argue it will protect the European asylum system while removing the incentive for irregular migrants to come to Europe.

Denmark’s immigration minister, Morten Bødskov, whose government has pioneered a tough line on migration widely emulated across the EU, said it was “a huge breakthrough”.

The German interior minister, Alexander Dobrindt, said: “Return hubs offer a positive approach for people without the right to stay and with no prospects in Europe, whose countries of origin do not cooperate in the repatriation process.”

Andy Burnham told reporters last week that the UK had not had direct discussions with European governments involved in return hubs but “we will watch that with interest, learning from what went on in the past. The public want us to do whatever we can to deal with illegal immigration.”

Critics have highlighted the ethical difficulties of striking an agreement with Rwanda’s repressive government, which was found in 2024 by the US state department to allow state security forces and civilian officials to commit human rights abuses with impunity, while also raising concerns about freedom of expression and forced labour.

Spain’s socialist prime minister, Pedro Sanchez, said on Monday the move had no “future or humanity”. “I don’t see any future or humanity in that. It’s not the solution,” Sanchez, an outspoken defender of immigration in a Europe that has tilted to the right in recent years, told Telecinco television.

The French Green MEP Mélissa Camara denounced the agreement between the five countries and Rwanda as “an agreement of shame” and “a grave moral failing”. She said: “Under this agreement, people will be deported thousands of kilometres away from the European Union, with no guarantees regarding the compliance with their rights.”

Alain Berset, the secretary general of the Council of Europe, the continent’s human rights body, said it was not possible “to be so precise a priori” about what any return hubs proposal meant. Speaking to the Guardian before the announcement, he said all members of the Council of Europe were committed to the European convention on human rights “and this must be the lighthouse” for all discussions on return hubs.

“On concrete cases, then we will have to see if this is compliant with the convention,” he said. He stressed that “all countries must be able to define their migration policy”.

Officials involved in setting up the migration centres expect legal challenges, but say the scheme is grounded in human rights law.

Rights organisations say the scheme is bound to fail. “Member states may call them ‘return hubs’ or even ‘welcome centres’, but no euphemism can hide the violence of uprooting people from the life they’ve been building in Europe, and sending them to a country they don’t even know, without family, networks or meaningful prospects,” said Chiara Catelli of Picum, a pan-European organisation that campaigns for the rights of undocumented migrants.

#9. Iran Fires Missiles and Drones at Gulf Arab States as Peace Talks Falter

Kuwait and Bahrain said they were targeted on Wednesday, a sign of how the Middle East is stuck in limbo between peace and full-blown war.

→ Baca artikel asli

#8. Kuwait says it repelled first Iranian attack in two months after US strikes island

Kuwait condemned the attack, which Iran said targeted an airbase hosting US forces (file photo)

Kuwait and Bahrain have said they repelled Iranian air attacks overnight as hostilities between Iran and the United States rise.

The drone attack on Kuwait was the first in more than two months. The Iranian army said it had targeted the Ali Al Salem Airbase, which hosts US forces, but Kuwaiti authorities did not report any damage.

It came after US warplanes struck an Iranian missile launch site on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, according to US military officials.

Meanwhile, the United Arab Emirates' state oil firm said a crew member was missing after one of its tankers was attacked in the Gulf waterway on Tuesday.

The recent exchanges of fire, as well as deadlock in negotiations on a peace deal, have raised fears of a major escalation in the nine-month-old war.

The conflict began when the US and Israel attacked Iran in late February.

Since then, Iran has retaliated for US and Israeli strikes by launching missiles and drones at Israel and Gulf Arab states hosting US military assets and personnel.

It has also attacked dozens of commercial vessels in and around the Strait of Hormuz, through which a fifth of global oil and gas shipments usually pass.

Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years for Iran-backed plot to kill Trump

Trump says US taking 'very seriously' any Iranian plot against Washington

Iran targets US military base in Bahrain with drone attack

On Tuesday night, Kuwait's military said its air defences engaged "drone threats" but did not report any casualties or damage.

The Kuwaiti foreign ministry condemned the attack, saying it "flagrantly violated Kuwait's sovereignty, directly threatened its security and stability, and constituted a grave breach of international law".

"The explosions seemed to be coming from far away, but hearing them again after a period of calm was both surprising and worrying," Hani Mohammed, an Egyptian resident of Kuwait, told AFP news agency.

Bahrain's military also said on Wednesday that its air defence systems had "intercepted and destroyed several Iranian aerial attacks".

The island kingdom, which hosts the US Navy's 5th Fleet, has reported four attacks over the past six days, following a two-month lull.

Qatar, which has mediated in the stalled indirect talks between Iran and the US, said the Iranian attacks on its neighbours were a "dangerous escalation that threatens to complicate efforts aimed at containing tensions".

The Iranian army said its drones had targeted US Patriot air defence systems and drone and missile launch platforms at Ali Al Salem Air Base in Kuwait.

Later, the army's commander-in-chief, Maj Gen Amir Hatami, warned the US and its allies that it would respond more forcefully to any new attacks on Iran.

"If the enemy repeats its mistake... we have the readiness and the plans to confront it and its supporters in a stronger, more crushing way and without geographic limitations," he said.

On Tuesday night, US fighter jets targeted a missile launch site on Qeshm Island "with precision munitions" in response to Iranian "aggression targeting US personnel and assets", US military officials said.

Iranian media reported explosions on the island but cited a local official as saying they had caused no damage to infrastructure.

In a separate development, the Abu Dhabi National Oil Company (Adnoc) said a search-and-rescue operation was under way for a missing sailor following an attack on one of its vessels in the Strait of Hormuz on Tuesday. The rest of the crew were safely evacuated and 11 were receiving medical care, it added.

The UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) centre reported that a tanker was struck by an unknown projectile about 13 nautical miles (24km) north of Khasab, in Oman's Musandam peninsula. It said the attack caused a fire on board that was extinguished.

Iran said two oil tankers and a ship carrying liquefied natural gas (LNG) had caught fire after hitting naval mines while sailing along an "authorised route".

At least 25 seafarers have been killed across the Middle East since the start of the war, when the Strait of Hormuz was effectively closed due to Iranian missile and drone attacks on commercial vessels and a US naval blockade of Iranian ports.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the conflict and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

Iran insisted it would not give up some measure of control over the waterway and began targeting vessels using a southern route through Omani waters that avoids its preferred route through Iranian waters.

In recent months, the US military has been providing protection for tankers shuttling through the Strait of Hormuz, allowing crude oil exports from Gulf states to reach around 80% of pre-war levels, according to analysts.
